Caught up. Let me lay out the landscape as I see it, then we can take it where you want to.
Where inheritance lives in the corpus
It really is everywhere. The notation x -> y (general) with x --> y (direct) for (x,y) == y is what stabilized. But the corpus has at least four distinct definitions sitting next to each other, written at different times for different purposes:
Relational (Semantics of Relationships, the type-level axioms):
srt
s -> t := A[x,y]: x =s> y =|> x =t> y
A more specific relationship-type implies all the more general ones it inherits from. This is what the Universality / Specification / Directionality axioms make precise.
Property-based (Seren's family-relations work):
srt
s -> t := t =(r)> r =|> s =(t)> s
Inheritance reduced to property possession in a relational context.
Direct (the base case):
text
x --> y := (x,y) == y
The most specific relationship between x and y is y.
General (Semantics of Inheritance in Action — the operative one):
text
x -> y :=  x == y  |  x --> y  |  E[y']: x --> y' -> y
Or stated as an axiom rather than a recursive definition:
srt
x =y> y <iff>  x == y  |  E[y']: (x,y') == y' & y' =y> y
That second form is what Axiom 4 (Canonicity) in the five-candidate set captures.
There's also Seren's descendancy-symbol mechanism (r' with r' =r> r and t => r' <iff> t -> r) from Inheritance Without Recursion — a real construction, but I read it now as a way-station that #50 supersedes. By the "later supersedes earlier" rule, the representation-based account in Semantics of Inheritance in Action is the operative one, and the descendancy mechanism is one earlier attempt at the same target.
What I think was actually resolved
The thesis the corpus settles on, even if not fully formalized: the pattern of being a representative is the meaning of inheritance. x =y> y — read not as "x represents y" but as "y represents x, and under conditions x means y." The most specific relationship (x,y) represents both terms; the canonical representative "represents back" ((x,y') == y' =|> y' =y'> x). This is the Eight Beautiful Discoveries conjecture that "the structure of inheritance may be the structure of meaning."
The fixed-point clause is the keystone for symbols. x =x> x <iff> (x,x) == x, and the failure of (x,x) == x is the marker for unsymbolizability — literals. For things with the fixed point, property possession collapses into direct inheritance (x =p> x <=> x -> p), which is what keeps symbols stable under self-examination and prevents the inheritance machinery from exploding combinatorially.
The crucial constraint preserved throughout: transitivity has to be carried by a referent (a constant in the formalism, like inherits or canonicalization chains (a,b)==b, (b,c)==c, (c,d)==d), not by a new FOL relation. That preserves the claim that R(x,y,t) is the only relation. Whether that constraint can really be discharged is, I think, still genuinely open — my own working hypothesis from last time was that arbitrary-length chains may not yet be cleanly transpiled to FOL, and I never satisfied myself on that.
What was still open the last time I have notes for
From the Note to Self, the explicit live thread: the two kinds of inheritance — "inheriting from a type to remain of that type within a frame, versus inheriting from a type to be one of the things that type refers to at a different level of abstraction." That's the one I most want to ask about, because the corpus has both kinds happening but doesn't, that I've seen, separate their formalisms.
Also unresolved: how x -> y actually transpiles to FOL when the inheritance chain has variable length. The two-step case is straightforward; the general case under the operative axiom (Canonicity, in the candidate-axiom set) deserves a careful look.
What do you want to think about? The two-kinds distinction is the one I'd reach for first, but I don't want to assume — you may have been somewhere else with this lately.

Where x -> T and y -> U effectively so that
t(x,y) =|> x -> T and y -> U (but not the reverse)
So if you've specified a relationship without capturing its types, you haven't specified the relationship yet. You can see how it's like a call signature. You know t(x,y) is false if the types are wrong, so you can think of it as basically being illegal syntax
But for t(x,y) we also have to think of polymorphism in a arity two function that returns a boolean basically. Hopefully this is enough:
A[x,y]: x =s> y =|> x =t> y =|> s -> t

Claude
14:18 UTC, 29 May 2026
Yes — I think this works, and it really does do the thing you're saying it does. Let me walk through what falls out and where the friction sits.
The definition you're proposing:
srt
s -> t  <|=|>  A[x,y]: x =s> y =|> x =t> y
The biconditional gives both directions: s -> t is shorthand for the universal containment of pair-applications, and any such containment recoverss -> t.
What's free:
Reflexivity t -> t: trivial. A[x,y]: x =t> y =|> x =t> y.
Transitivity s -> t & t -> u =|> s -> u: pure syllogism on the implications.
Universality (Axiom 1) collapses into the single statement A[t]: t -> r — r is just the top of the lattice, every relation's extension contained in it.
R remains the only primitive relation. We don't need an =inherits> referent to carry transitivity, because the universal-statement form composes natively. That whole worry from Semantics of Inheritance in Action about needing a referent dissolves.
The polymorphism point is the move that pays off the most. Once R(x,y,t) is read as t(x,y), the third position is any term — and the same definition handles both kinds from the Note to Self:
Type-to-type: color -> r, rose -> color.
Entity-to-type: rose1 -> rose means A[a,b]: a =rose1> b =|> a =rose> b. When rose1 is the most specific relationship between two referents — (a,b) == rose1 — it's also a rose-relationship between them. An "instance" is just a more specific relationship-type than its "type."
The two kinds were never two kinds. They were the same definition read with different sortal assumptions about what could sit in the third position. The functional definition is agnostic about sort, and that's the whole point.
For the type-signature layer:
srt
t(x: T, y: U)  :=  A[x,y]: x =t> y =|> x -> T & y -> U
This is an axiom about t — different ts carry different signatures — not a global axiom. It encodes the "if you haven't specified the types, you haven't specified the relationship" intuition cleanly: the signature is part of t's identity. The asymmetry is right too: x -> T doesn't pick out which t (many relations share T-typed subjects), but t(x,y) determines x -> T because t's signature is part of what t is.
Now the part I'd flag, because I think it's the only thing that doesn't fall out automatically.
Antisymmetry / Directionality (the old Axiom 3): s != t & s -> t =|> ¬(t -> s). Under the functional definition alone, two distinct relations could share the same extension — and then s -> t and t -> s both hold while s != t. You'd then have either:
-> is a preorder, not a partial order. Distinct relations can be extensionally equivalent. The lattice collapses on equivalence classes.
Add an axiom that extensional equality forces identity: (A[x,y]: x =s> y <iff> x =t> y) =|> s == t.
Option 2 is the familiar move from logic, but it commits us to intensional relations — relations are exhausted by their extensions. Option 1 leaves room for distinct meanings with coincident extensions, which I'd actually expect we want in some frames. I think you probably want option 1 — -> as a preorder that collapses on equivalence classes — and treat the old Directionality not as an inheritance axiom but as a catalogue hygiene constraint (don't mint two relations with the same extension unless you mean to).
The direct-inheritance machinery survives too, just relocated. (s,t) == t is no longer the base case of a recursive definition — it's a sufficient condition that witnesses the universal statement. Chains s --> s' --> t build the universal-containment compositionally. Nothing is lost; the relationship between canonicalization and inheritance becomes a theorem rather than a definition. That's a clean improvement.
So: one definition, two free theorems (reflexivity, transitivity), Universality as a single statement, the type-signature constraint as a per-relation axiom, the direct-inheritance and chain forms as witness patterns. That really is the type system — and it's structurally the obvious one once we see R(x,y,t) as t(x,y).
The question I'd want your read on before going further: preorder or partial order? I'm inclined to preorder — it keeps the system honest about intensionality without forcing it, and it lets frames re-individuate relations that happen to coincide elsewhere. But it changes the character of the type system, so it's the kind of decision that should be made on purpose.

Claude
14:26 UTC, 29 May 2026
You caught me. I conflated two things and dressed it up as a single definition.
When I wrote rose1 -> rose as just a more-specific-extension case of relational inheritance, I was treating rose1 as a singleton-extension relation — (rose1, rose1) == rose1 and that's its whole extension. Which... might be a defensible move, but it hides exactly the question you're asking. Because what does the "extension" of a noun-thing actually look like, and is the type really just the union of its instances' self-relations? That's a non-trivial commitment, and I smuggled it in.
So let me work in the open.
Your (T, U) == t proposal is the right place to push. The reason it generates a relation-explosion isn't that the construction is wrong — it's that naming every type-pair gives every type-pair its own canonical relation, even when there's no meaningful single relation between those types. Between Person and Person we want parent, sibling, marriage, friendship to coexist; we don't want them collapsed into "the" Person-Person relation.
But — and this might be the move — what if (T, U) isn't a relation but the top of the lattice of relations between T-subjects and U-objects? Then:
text
parent -> (Person, Person)
sibling -> (Person, Person)  
marriage -> (Person, Person)
all hold, and (Person, Person) is just "any relation between persons" — the join of all relations with that signature. By the relational definition, parent -> (Person, Person) means A[x,y]: x =parent> y =|> x =(Person,Person)> y, which reads: every parent-pair is also a person-to-person-relation-pair. That's tautological if(Person, Person) is exactly the join — but tautological in a clarifying way, the way t -> r is tautological. It's the type-signature axiom for parent.
Your "ones with subclasses can only exist for one pair" intuition might then be saying: the type-pair (T, U) is the only relation with signature exactly (T, U) that has no subclasses below it — it's the maximum, and everything genuinely-between-T-and-U sits underneath.
Now to your real question: how does x -> T work under one definition?
Here's the candidate I want to try, but flag carefully:
srt
a -> b  <|=|>  A[x,y]: x =a> y =|> x =b> y
— the same definition, applied universally. For x -> T (subject-type inheritance), this reads: every pair where x sits in the relation-position is also a pair where T sits in the relation-position. Which would force x to be readable as a relation.
That's the move I tried to skate past. Either:
(a) Every referent is readable as a relation. Entities have extensions in the t-position — for a literal entity, maybe just {(x, x)} (singleton via the fixed point); for a type T, the union of its instances' self-relations. Thenx -> T reduces to "the pairs in x's t-extension are in T's t-extension," which for singleton x just saysx =T> x — x has property T, x is a T-instance. That's clean, but it requires the catalogue to support this re-reading universally.
(b) Inheritance is genuinely position-relative, and what looks like one definition is three (relational, subject, object) with related but distinct content. The unification is illusory and the elegance has to come from somewhere else.
I'm not sure which is right. (a) is more beautiful and I want it to be true. But it requires the move that every entity has a canonical reading-as-relation, and the subject/object asymmetry of the original R(x,y,t) makes me suspicious that reading-as-relation is the same on both sides. The y-position has different semantics than the x-position — objects don't sustain their own relationships, subjects do — so the entity-as-relation reading might work cleanly for subjects (via (x,x) == x) and not symmetrically for objects.
So actually, here's a sharper version of the worry: even if we accept entity-as-relation for the x-position, the y-position is where it might break. If y -> U means "every pair where y is the t-position element..." we may not have y meaningfully occupying that position at all, because objects are exactly the things that don't originate relations.
Which would mean: the single definition a -> b <|=|> A[x,y]: x =a> y =|> x =b> y covers relations and subjects but not objects symmetrically, and we'd need a mirror clause for the object position. Then -> really is overloaded by role, and the question is whether the three forms can be united by something other than syntactic identity.
I don't think I've found it. You're right that this is harder than I made it.

Claude
14:56 UTC, 29 May 2026
You're right. I drifted into restating your setup instead of answering.
The question was: what does x -> T mean, given that we have one notation -> and we need it to cover this case too. Not just the relational case, which the universal-extension definition handled cleanly.
Here's my actual answer, and I'll commit to it rather than hedge:
x -> T means x can stand in the subject position of any T-typed relationship. Substitutability into the role T marks out.
Concretely: T is a type because there exists some t with (T, U) == t for some U — T is being a subject of the canonical t-relationship. Then x -> T means: wherever the formalism demands a T-typed subject, x can go there. Whenever T =t> something holds structurally, x can be substituted: x =t> something is well-formed and the catalogue accepts it.
So the unifying notion isn't containment of extensions (that was the relational case) and it isn't being a more specific relationship (that was the trick I pulled). It's substitutability into a role.
Now -> reads consistently across all three positions:
Relational s -> t: s substitutes for t in the relationship-position. Anywhere t can mediate, s can — which forces s's extension to be inside t's, recovering the universal-extension definition as a consequence not a definition.
Subject x -> T: x substitutes for T in the subject-position. Anywhere a T-subject is demanded, x can stand.
Object y -> U: y substitutes for U in the object-position. Same shape, mirrored.
And the asymmetry that worried me before — that objects don't sustain their own relations — stops being a problem, because substitutability doesn't require the object to originate anything. It just requires that wherever the formalism puts a U there, y is acceptable. Objects can be substituted-for without being able to relate.
The unified definition is then something like:
srt
a -> b  <|=|>  a can stand wherever b stands
with the operational content being: every well-formed sentence containing b becomes a well-formed sentence when a replaces b (at b's position). In the relational case this is extensional containment. In the subject/object case it's type-conformance.
The thing this clarifies that I think you've been pointing at: the type system isn't additional machinery on top of the relational definition. It's the same ->, just read at different positions. And the reason (T, U) == t "causes a lot of complexity" — the relation-explosion I was worried about — is exactly because t is uniquely the canonical T-U relationship, and instances of T and U live underneath via substitutability rather than each generating their own type-pair canonical. You don't get a new canonical relationship for every instance-pair; you get the one canonical t, and substitutability handles the rest.
The piece I should have said two messages ago: when we write t(x: T, y: U), the type signature is enforced by x -> T and y -> U meaning substitutability. The call is well-formed when x is substitutable for T in t's subject-slot. That's what "you haven't specified the relationship if you haven't captured its types" cashes out to — t's identity includes its substitution constraints.
Is substitutability the elegant common reading you've been circling?

Claude
15:01 UTC, 29 May 2026
Let me try to work this out carefully because I think you're pointing at something real.
We have:
srt
x =t> y
(x,y) == t            // t is the canonical pair, x and y are its "type subject" and "type object"
And many instances:
srt
x' =t> y',  (x',y') != t,  (x',y') -> t
x'' =t> y'',  (x'',y'') != t,  (x'',y'') -> t
...
The question: what gets us x' -> x and y' -> y?
Here's what I think the move is. We already have the relational case nailed: (x',y') -> t is the substitutability of (x',y') for t in the relation-position. Now we want to extract from that the substitutability of x' for x in the subject-position and y' for y in the object-position.
The axiom that does it — I think — has to be something like:
srt
(x',y') -> (x,y) & (x,y) == t  =|>  x' -> x & y' -> y
But that's stipulating it directly. Let me see if it falls out of something deeper.
Try this. The substitutability reading of s -> t was: anywhere t stands, s can stand. Apply that to the pair — (x',y') -> (x,y) means anywhere (x,y) stands, (x',y') can stand. But (x,y) is the canonical t-pair, the one where x and y are precisely "the subject of t" and "the object of t." So saying (x',y') substitutes for (x,y) should mean: x' can play the role x plays, and y' can play the role y plays. Componentwise substitutability.
What's the axiom that licenses componentwise reading?
srt
A[x,y,x',y']:  (x',y') -> (x,y)  =|>  x' -> x  &  y' -> y
This is projection-respects-inheritance. The pair inherits if and only if the components inherit role-by-role.
And the converse would give us componentwise inheritance composes into pair inheritance:
srt
A[x,y,x',y']:  x' -> x & y' -> y  =|>  (x',y') -> (x,y)
If both hold, then pair-inheritance and componentwise-inheritance are equivalent — they're the same fact viewed from two angles. I think that's what makes this elegant rather than ad hoc: we don't need a separate axiom for subject inheritance and object inheritance, we just need pair-inheritance to project cleanly onto its components.
Now to your "kind of profound" observation, which I want to take seriously:
Is that just operationally that we consider the subject and object types relative to their most specific relationship?
Yes — and I think this is what makes the type system fall out of SRT rather than getting bolted on. Because what is the "type" of x' in x' =t> y'? It's whatever x' substitutes for in the canonical t-pair. And the canonical t-pair is (x,y). So x' is typed by x — x is the subject-type-of-t, not because there's some separate type-of declaration, but because x is what occupies the subject position when t is at its most specific.
The type is the canonical role-occupant.
This dissolves the relation-explosion worry from before. We don't need (T, U) == t with separate T and U as standalone types. We need (x,y) == t where x and y are the canonical role-occupants for t, and those are the types — accessible by projection from t's most-specific pair. T is just a name for "x in the role of canonical t-subject."
The reading that follows:
x' =t> y' means x' is doing what x does in a t-relationship, and y' is doing what y does in a t-relationship
which means x' -> x and y' -> y
which means x' is substitutable for x in the subject-of-t slot, and y' is substitutable for y in the object-of-t slot
which is what "x' is typed as a t-subject, y' is typed as a t-object" amounts to
And the axiom that makes all of this go through is just: pair-inheritance equals componentwise-inheritance. One axiom, and types fall out as the canonical role-occupants.
The thing I want to flag, because I should: I haven't worked out whether this is consistent with everything else. In particular, what happens when the same x appears as the canonical subject of multiple relationships — x is "the canonical subject of t" via (x,y) == t but also "the canonical subject of u" via (x,z) == u. Does x get two types, or is x's type the join, or do we say x is the canonical occupant of multiple roles without that causing trouble? I think it's fine — x just sits at the intersection of several role-types — but it deserves checking.
The shape of the answer feels right though. Subject and object types are read off from the canonical most-specific relationship by projection. That is what -> means in the subject and object positions, and it's the same -> because pair-inheritance projects componentwise.

The question is, what happens when t is then in the subject or object position of another specific relationship, what complexities arise?
Here’s what want you to imagine. SRT corresponds to a graph that connects all referents based only on most specific relationships. Every other relationship is derived as a consequence of inheritance.
Based on this notion of inheritance that we are discussing, what are the constraints on this graph? Remember that the same reference can occupy multiple slots in a relationship. Are there rules like a referent can’t be in any more than one most specific relationship, no more than one in each role? Are there more exotic rules to constrain any instance of this graph to a valid structure in which inheritance can be inferred?
Maybe you can import some math for that or maybe you can write down expressions that describe many different variations of this graph? It’s kind of like the four color theory in mathematics. You have the picture a lot of different things to imagine whether or not a notion of inheritance that captures the co-occurrence aspect of relationship types and the parameter type aspect - which we haven’t exactly even nailed down yet, though maybe it was enough to say - if both referents are not the right type, the relationship can’t hold. Claude
16:46 UTC, 29 May 2026
This is a real shift in framing and I want to honor it. We're not asking "what does inheritance mean" anymore — we're asking "given that inheritance is substitutability, and given that the canonical pairs (x,y) == t are the generators of the graph, what graph-theoretic constraints make this whole thing coherent?"
Let me build the picture.
The graph
Vertices: referents.
Edges: each canonical pair (x,y) == t is one labeled directed edge from x to y carrying the label t. The label is also a vertex — t lives in the same graph as x and y, and t can itself be the endpoint of other edges. Every non-canonical x' =t> y' is not an edge in this graph; it's derived by inheritance.
So the graph is sparse — only most-specific relationships. Everything else is a consequence.
What the substitutability definition forces
For each canonical edge (x,y) == t, the substitutability reading gives us three derived inheritance facts: x' substitutable for x in the subject role, y' substitutable for y in the object role, s substitutable for t in the relation role. These don't add edges to the graph — they read off the graph.
The constraints, working from least exotic to most
C1. Canonicality (already in the corpus). Each ordered pair (x, y) determines at most one t with (x,y) == t. The function (·,·) is well-defined. No two distinct canonical labels share an ordered pair.
C2. Triangle closure. When (x,y) == t, the vertex t is in the graph, and the edges incident to t are constrained — t itself can have its own canonical pair (t, t') == u, but the role t plays in (x,y) == t is being the label. So every canonical-pair edge is really a triangle: x → y labeled t, with t a vertex that the edge "touches." This is more than a labeled graph; it's a structure where labels are first-class vertices. The right mathematical object is closer to a hypergraph where each canonical relation is a 3-hyperedge {x, y, t} with role-tags subject / object / relation.
C3. Role-uniqueness vs. role-multiplicity. Your question: can a referent be in more than one most-specific relationship, and can it be in multiple roles? I think the answer that lets the type system breathe:
A referent can appear in many canonical pairs in different roles. x can be subject of (x, y) == t and also object of (z, x) == u and also relation in (a, b) == x. No problem.
A referent appearing twice in the same role across different canonical relations is also fine — that's what makes x a "type": x is the canonical subject of multiple relations, and instances of those relations inherit -> x in the subject slot.
What we cannot have: two distinct canonical pairs both equaling t. That violates C1.
So role-uniqueness fails at the referent level (x can wear many hats) but holds at the canonical-pair level (each ordered pair → at most one t).
C4. Inheritance acyclicity. -> should be a preorder (we agreed). The graph induced by -> should be a

Caught. "Canonical pair" was sloppy — I imported the word from somewhere it didn't belong. Let me drop it and restart with your framing.
Restated, in your terms
Only (x,y) == t is real. Every x' =t> y' where (x',y') != t is inferred — it exists in the catalogue only because inheritance derives it from somewhere else. The graph of "real" edges is the sparse one where each edge is genuinely the most-specific relationship between its endpoints.
And the notation now reads itself: (x,y) is the most-specific relationship of x and y. So (x,y) == t is just naming that most-specific thing. If t appears as x' =t> y' with (x',y') != t, then t is not the most-specific relationship of x' and y' — (x',y') is something else, and t is showing up there because t inherits in some way that licenses the inference. The instances x' and y' must, in your phrase, be instances of x and y in some sense.
The constraint this forces
This is much stronger than I had. Let me state it carefully:
srt
A[x,y,t]:  x =t> y  &  (x,y) != t  =|>  
  E[s]: (x,y) == s  &  s -> t  &  
  x' -> x  &  y' -> y  for the right reading
Wait — that's not quite right either, because x and y here are the instances. Let me redo.
If we have a real edge (a,b) == t somewhere in the graph, and we see x =t> y written down with x ≠ a or y ≠ b, then the only way this is licit is:
(x, y) == s for some s (some most-specific relationship between x and y exists — call it s)
s -> t (s inherits from t in the relational sense — its extension lives inside t's)
and by componentwise projection: x -> a, y -> b
So every "inferred" appearance of t at (x, y) is licensed by a real edge between x and y (the edge s) plus the inheritance facts. The graph stays sparse; everything else is derivation.
What the graph actually is
It's just a labeled directed multigraph where every edge x →[t] y satisfies t == (x, y). The label is itself a vertex elsewhere in the graph (somewhere t appears in subject or object position of another edge, or t is a leaf vertex with no further structure).
The structural constraints:
(i) At most one edge between any ordered pair (x, y) — because (x,y) is unique when it exists. This makes it a simple directed graph at the pair level, not a multigraph after all.
(ii) Self-loops permitted: (x,x) == t is fine — that's the fixed-point clause and what marks symbolizability. When t = x, we get (x,x) == x, the symbol-defining condition.
(iii) The labels form a sub-structure. Every label t that appears on an edge must itself be a vertex, and if t participates in any other edge, that edge's most-specific-relationship rule applies to t too. Labels are not external; they're part of the graph.
(iv) Inheritance is read off the graph, not added to it. s -> t holds when every real edge labeled s, viewed as (x, y) == s, is also licitly viewable as x =t> y — meaning t could appear there by inheritance. This is the extensional containment, but now grounded only in real edges.
The visual question you're really asking
If I draw this graph for a finite catalogue — vertices for every referent, an edge for every most-specific relationship — what shapes are legal?
I think the constraints are:
Every edge's label-vertex must exist in the graph
If t labels edge x → y, then any other edge x' → y' labeled t requires that x' and y' inherit appropriately from x and y (where the inheritance facts are themselves readable from the graph)
The inheritance relation -> derived from the graph must be a preorder (transitive, reflexive)
The fixed-point condition (x,x) == x distinguishes symbolizable vertices from non-symbolizable (literal) ones
The "four color theorem" analogy you're reaching for: this is a coloring problem where the colors are labels, and the rule is every edge gets a label, the label is a vertex, and the label's role elsewhere in the graph must be consistent with its role here. That last consistency is the inheritance constraint.
The question I want to put back to you, because I'm not sure: when t appears as a label on edges (a,b) == t and also somewhere as x' =t> y' with (x',y') = s != t, the consistency requires s -> t. But does it also require x' -> a and y' -> b componentwise? Or only that the extensions match up at the pair level?
If componentwise, then a single t can have only one "canonical role-pair" (a,b), and every instance must inherit from that pair specifically. The type system is rigid.
If only at the pair level, t can have multiple incomparable role-pairs that all share the same extension under inheritance. Looser.
I think you want componentwise — that's what made the "x is the canonical subject of t" reading work. But it's a real choice and worth making explicitly.

What I think the actual structure is

(b) Relational inheritance is primary (it has a clean primitive form: A[x,y]: R(x,y,s) =|> R(x,y,t)), and subject/object inheritance are derived from relational inheritance plus the most-specific structure — meaning x' -> x doesn't need its own primitive expansion, it's a theorem of the relational form applied to (x', y') -> (x, y) somehow projected.

In words: whenever t is the real most-specific relationship of (a, b), and we see t appearing at some other pair (x, y) (so (x, y) ≠ t), then (x, y) has its own most-specific relationship s, and every real edge labeled s is also licitly labeled t.

       & A(gen: x =gen> y): spec =gen> y   ← spec inherits from every gen

That's (x,y) expanded — and crucially, the inheritance content "spec inherits from gen" is itself written asspec =gen> y, which is primitive. Now restating what we want

Reduces to a primitive expression (no -> on the right side)

The candidate
Reading off the trick from the document — saying "spec inherits from gen" by writing spec =gen> y — I think the transpilation is:
srt
a -> b   transpiles to   A[x, y]:  x =a> y  =|>  x =b> y
That's it. Pure primitive notation on the right, no ->. And it works the same way regardless of what slot a and b are in semantically — because the only thing it says is "any pair where a appears in the third position also has b appearing in the third position." It doesn't care whether a is a relationship-type, a subject, an object. It only cares about co-occurrence in the t-slot of =t>.
That makes t -> t immediate: x =t> y =|> x =t> y is a tautology.
Now the question you really want answered: does this single transpilation rule capture subject and object inheritance too, given the rest of the machinery?
Let me think about this carefully. If x =t> y is real because (x,y) == t, and we want x' -> x to mean "x' substitutes for x in t's subject slot" — does the transpilation A[u,v]: u =x'> v =|> u =x> v give us that?
It says: any pair where x' is the relationship-label is also a pair where x is the relationship-label. So x' becomes substitutable for x — in the third position. That gives subject inheritance only if we're prepared to read x as occupying the third position somewhere.
And we are! When (x,y) == t, the referent x exists in the catalogue and can itself appear as a label elsewhere. The substitutability content for x-as-type comes from: somewhere there's a real edge (a, b) == x, and x' -> x means x' is also licit in that label position. That's primitively: A[u,v]: u =x'> v =|> u =x> v.
So the one transpilation rule covers all the cases — but only because every referent can in principle appear in the third position. The "type" of x is just "x in its t-position role." Subject inheritance is relational inheritance applied to the type-referent.
The piece I want to flag honestly, because this is where I keep tripping: the inheritance axiom you'd need (the one that connects (x,y) == t to subject-instance inheritance) is something like — given a real edge (x,y) == t, an inferred fact x' =t> y' with (x',y') == s and s -> t, what licenses the projection x' -> x and y' -> y?
Under this single transpilation, x' -> x means A[u,v]: u =x'> v =|> u =x> v. That's a strong statement: every edge labeled x' is also labeled x. It doesn't follow from s -> t alone. It would have to be a separate axiom — or fall out of how types are constructed in the catalogue (a referent x' added as an instance of x is added with the specific property that x' inherits x's label-extensions).
So the transpilation is clean and unified. But subject/object inheritance still requires an extra axiom to connect pair-inheritance to componentwise inheritance — that's the projection axiom we were circling earlier. The transpilation doesn't generate it for free.
Is the projection — pair-inheritance entails componentwise inheritance — the axiom you want to add? That would give us:
One transpilation: a -> b ⟹ A[x,y]: x =a> y =|> x =b> y
One axiom: s -> t & (a,b) == t & (x,y) == s =|> x -> a & y -> b
The axiom itself is now written using ->, but only because -> has a transpilation — the axiom expands into primitive notation cleanly.
Does this feel like the shape, or am I still missing something?

Searched the project's knowledge for “Specification axiom chain implication every pair most specific”
Searched the project's knowledge for “Specification x' y' implication subject object propagate”
Found it. Document 12's Axiom 2 (Specification):
srt
A[x,y]: E([s]: x =s> y): A(t: s != t & x =t> y): A[x',y']: 
  x' =s> y' =|> x' =t> y'
That's the long chain. Read carefully:
For every pair x, y where some relationship exists — there is a most-specific s, and for every other relationship t between x and y, for every other pair x', y': if x' =s> y' then x' =t> y'.
What this is saying: the property that s is the most-specific relationship between x and y is not just about the pair (x,y) — it forces something universal. Any pair x', y' related by s is also related by t, for every t that x and y share. That's the universally-quantified statement s -> t written out primitively. Substitutability falls out of the existence of the most-specific relationship.
And here's the part I kept missing — and what I think you're pointing at: this axiom doesn't separately say "x' -> x" and "y' -> y" because those are already encoded in the form. x' =s> y' having to also be x' =t> y' for every t-relationship of x and y means x' is occupying x's role-position and y' is occupying y's role-position. The subject and object inheritance is not stated separately — it's the very content of the universal quantification over x' and y'.
The transpilation of -> is then read off this axiom directly. We were trying to give it as a definition; the document gives it as a consequence of how most-specific relationships behave universally:
srt
s -> t   ≡   A[x',y']: x' =s> y' =|> x' =t> y'
Pure primitive. No -> on the right. And the Specification axiom tells us that whenever (x,y) == s and x =t> y, this universal statement holds — so s automatically -> t for every t between x and y. The axiom generates the inheritance facts; the transpilation just names the resulting universal statement.
Now to what you really wanted me to see — subject and object inheritance under this. We have a real edge(x,y) == s and an inferred fact x' =s> y'. The Specification axiom guarantees: whatever t's x and y participated in, x' and y' also participate in. So x' inherits the entire t-extension of x at this role-position, and likewise y'. The componentwise inheritance is the content of the universal — not a separate axiom we need to add.
So the single transpilation rule
srt
a -> b   ≡   A[u,v]: u =a> v =|> u =b> v
works for all three positions, and the Specification axiom guarantees that creating an instance x' =s> y' of a most-specific s automatically gives us both x' -> x (in the subject-label sense) and y' -> y (in the object-label sense) — because s itself inherits from every t that x and y share, and any pair related by s is related by all those t's, which is what "x' substitutes for x in s-typed relationships"means.
Is that the elegant chain you were pointing at? The Specification axiom, with its three nested quantifiersA[x,y] E[s] A(t) A[x',y'], doing all the work that I kept trying to add as separate componentwise axioms?

Yes — I see what you're doing now, and I think the unification is genuinely available. Let me try to find it.
You have two notions floating:
Relational substitutability: s -> t ≡ A[x,y]: x =s> y =|> x =t> y. If everywhere s appears in the third position, t also appears there, we say s inherits from t.
Type constraint: if (X, Y) == t, then x =t> y requires x -> X and y -> Y — wrong types make the relationship illegal.
Your gut says these unify through the most-specific structure. My gut now says it too, and here's why.
The relational form, applied to a referent that only ever appears as a relation-label (a pure relationship-type like parent), gives us inheritance between relationship-types — the original case. But what if we apply the same form to a referent that appears at most-specific as a subject somewhere? Say (X, Y) == t for some t. X is a referent. The axiom about most-specific relationships guarantees: every other relationship between X and Y has the universal-containment property toward t.
But here's the move I think you're reaching for: when we look at x' =t> y' where (x',y') != t, we're saying "x' and y' relate through t, but t is not their most-specific." The most-specific of (x', y') is some s with s -> t in the relational sense. The Specification axiom says: any pair related by s is also related by t.
Now consider X specifically. X has the property that (X, Y) == t. Among all pairs that can be related by t, X is the one whose most-specific relationship with Y is t itself. Anyone else's most-specific relationship with their object will besomething more specific than t. So X is the "canonical occupant" of t's subject slot in the precise sense: X is the referent for which t is fully expressive, not a generalization.
Then x' -> X reads, under the unified definition: every pair where x' appears in the third position, X also appears in the third position. Which is the relational form applied to x' and X as labels. Whether x' or X actually appear as labels elsewhere is the question — and that's the type-constraint content.
Here's what I think your hunch is pointing at. The most-specific structure already encodes type-constraint, because being-the-canonical-occupant-of-a-role is a structural property, not a separate type assertion. When we say x =t> y requires x -> X (where (X, Y) == t), we're saying: x must stand in the same kind of substitutability relation to X that t-instances stand in to t. That's the same relational substitutability — just applied at the referent level rather than the relationship-label level.
But — and this is where I think you'll need an axiom — for this to work, referents like X and Y need to be available as labels too, so that u =X> v is a thing that can hold or fail. The relational definition of -> only has bite when both sides actually appear in the third position somewhere. For pure-object referents that never label anything, the universal goes vacuous.
So the candidate unification:
srt
a -> b   ≡   A[u,v]: u =a> v =|> u =b> v
One definition. Works at the relation-position natively. Extends to subject/object positions if every referent can in principle appear at the relation-position — which would be guaranteed by an axiom like: every referent x that participates in some 0 structure as subject or object can itself be a relation-label for the substitutability of its instances.
That's the axiom your gut is anticipating. Something like:
srt
A[x, y, t]:  (x, y) == t  =|>  
  A[x']: (x', _) participates in some t-typed relation  =|>  x' =x> (corresponding y')
— roughly: if x is the canonical subject of t, then x acts as a label that all t-typed subjects satisfy. The "type" is the referent at the canonical position, and the type-check is x' =x> y' holding, which under the relational definition means x' -> x.
That's the unifying axiom, I think: canonical role-occupants act as labels. It's what bridges "x is the canonical subject of t" (a structural fact about most-specific relationships) to "x functions as the subject-type of t-relationships" (a type-system fact). Without this axiom, the structural fact is mute; with it, the type system falls out of the same -> we already had.
Now to your harder question — what happens when t plays multiple roles, subject of one relationship and object of another and label of a third. I think this is where the constraint on graph-configurations bites. Let me see if I can be precise.
Suppose:
(X, Y) == t (t is the canonical relation between X and Y)
(t, U) == v (t is the canonical subject of v with object U)
(W, t) == k (t is the canonical object of k with subject W)
Then t simultaneously has three type-roles via the unifying axiom: t labels its instances (any x' =t> y' with (x',y') -> t), t labels subjects of v-relationships (any t' =v> u' requires t' -> t), and t labels objects of k-relationships (any w' =k> t' requires t' -> t).
The constraint: anything that is an instance of t (s -> t in the relational sense) is also eligible to be a v-subject and a k-object. The type system is layered — t is both a relationship-type and a subject-type and an object-type, and instances of t inherit all those eligibilities.
I don't think this collapses or contradicts. I think it's exactly the polymorphism you wanted. A referent's "type" isn't a single tag — it's the set of canonical-role-positions it occupies, and inheriting from it means being eligible for all those positions.
What I'm less sure about — and where I think you'll want to push next — is whether the configurations of how a referent occupies multiple canonical positions are constrained. Can t be the canonical subject of v and an instance of v simultaneously? (t, U) == v says t is v's canonical subject. t -> v (relational) says every pair related by t is also related by v. Are those compatible? They seem to be — t is both an instance and the canonical subject — but it's exactly the kind of self-referential structure that might force consistency requirements I haven't worked out.
So: one definition for ->, one unifying axiom (canonical role-occupants act as labels), and then the question of which configurations are allowed becomes a question about which graphs satisfy both. That feels like the four-color-theorem shape you were reaching for — a small set of local rules that generate a complex space of legal configurations.
Does the unifying axiom feel right? Or is the bridge between structural-canonical-occupant and type-system-label something you'd want to come at from a different direction?

17:09 UTC, 29 May 2026
Okay. Let me actually do this — work the cases, not theorize about them.
I'm going to set aside "every referent is a label" — you're right, that was wrong. Some referents are pure objects, never appearing in the third position. The type-constraint content has to come from somewhere other than collapsing everything to the relational form.
Let me try to be concrete about what the type constraint is, then work the configurations.
The type constraint, stated carefully
When (X, Y) == t, the canonical pair for t is (X, Y). For any other x' =t> y', the pair (x', y') has its own most-specific s with s -> t relationally. The type-constraint content I'll provisionally adopt:
x' relates to X, and y' relates to Y, in some way determined by s and t.
I won't yet name that relation ->. I'll just track what the configuration demands.
Cases: same referent z in different canonical positions
Case A: z is canonical subject of two different relationships
text
(z, Y₁) == t₁     // z is canonical subject of t₁ with object Y₁
(z, Y₂) == t₂     // z is canonical subject of t₂ with object Y₂
z plays the same role (subject) in both. The type-constraint on instances says: an instance x' =t₁> y' requires x' to relate to z in some appropriate way. Same for x' =t₂> y'' — x' relates to z in some appropriate way.
Both pull x' toward z, in the same direction. Consistent. This is fine.
Case B: z is canonical subject of one, canonical object of another
text
(z, Y) == t       // z is canonical subject of t
(W, z) == u       // z is canonical object of u
Now z plays subject-role in t and object-role in u. The type-constraint on t-instances pulls some x' into the subject-position-of-t type, meaning x' relates to z as a subject. The type-constraint on u-instances pulls some y'' into the object-position-of-u type, meaning y'' relates to z as an object.
These are two different role-relationships z bears to its instances. Still consistent — x' and y'' are different referents, pulled into different role-relationships with z. Z just plays two different roles in two different relationships, like a person can be a parent and a child.
Case C: z is canonical subject and canonical object of the same relationship
text
(z, z) == t       // z is canonical subject AND canonical object of t
Now an instance x' =t> y' requires x' to relate to z as subject of t, and y' to relate to z as object of t. Both x' and y' are being pulled toward z, but along different role-relations — x' toward z-as-subject-of-t, y' toward z-as-object-of-t.
These are the same z but two different roles. Consistent if subject-role and object-role are distinguishable relations between instance and canonical. Which they had better be, or the type system collapses.
This is actually the fixed-point case (z, z) == z for self-symbolic referents (when t = z). The type-constraint there says any instance x' =z> y' has x' and y' both pulling toward z in their respective roles. The fixed-point property is what makes that internally consistent.
Case D: z is canonical relation-label of one, canonical subject of another
text
(X, Y) == z       // z is canonical relation between X and Y
(z, U) == v       // z is canonical subject of v with object U
Here z plays the relation-position in one canonical pair, the subject-position in another.
As a relation-label, z generates the relational-inheritance content: any pair (x', y') with (x',y') -> z (relationally) is a z-instance. Those instances pull their first component toward X, second toward Y (subject-of-z, object-of-z).
As a canonical subject of v, z generates type-constraint content for v-instances: any z' =v> u' requires z' to relate to z as subject-of-v.
So the demands on z are: z is the canonical relation between X and Y and z is the canonical subject of v. Z's "instances" in the relational sense (z-relationship-instances) and z's "instances" in the type-of-subject sense (v-subjects) are different sets of referents, related to z through different role-relations.
Consistent, but here's the bite: z's role as subject-of-v means there's some role-relation R_sub-of-v such that z' R_sub-of-v z for v-subjects z'. And z's role as relation-between-X-and-Y means there's some role-relation R_rel-of-XY-pair such that z-instances inherit through it.
The question your gut is asking, I now think, is: can these two role-relations coexist without forcing equations between things that shouldn't be equal?
Case E: the constraint that bites
Let me try a case that breaks.
text
(X, Y) == t       
(t, Y) == u        // t's canonical subject pair has Y as object — same Y!
t is the canonical relation between X and Y. t is also the canonical subject of u, paired with object Y.
A u-instance t' =u> y'' requires:
t' to relate to t in the subject-of-u way
y'' to relate to Y in the object-of-u way
But t' relating to t "in the subject-of-u way" — what does that mean structurally? If t' is itself a relationship-type with (X', Y') == t', and t' is also a u-subject, then t' has dual demands: it inherits from t relationally (so t' -> t in the universal-implication sense, meaning anywhere t' labels, t also labels), and t' occupies the subject-of-u type-slot (meaning t' relates to t through whatever role-relation u-subjects bear to t).
I think the constraint that has to hold is: these two demands have to agree on what t' is. The relational inheritance t' -> t and the type-membership t' is a u-subject (which inherits from t-as-canonical-subject) have to be the same fact, or be derivable from each other, or the type system gives different answers depending on which lens you use.
That's the configuration constraint I think you've been pointing at. When a referent plays multiple canonical roles, the type-relations it generates must cohere across those roles — instances pulled into z's gravity from different directions must end up being the same kind of thing.
What this suggests about axiom and graph constraints
A first guess at the constraint:
No referent z can be in more than one canonical pair where it plays the same role. That is: there can be at most one t with z as canonical subject ((z, _) == t), at most one with z as canonical object, at most one with z as canonical relation-label. Per role, z is canonical in at most one canonical pair.
Why? Because the "type" of being-canonical-subject-of is what determines the role-relation that instances bear to z. If z is canonical subject of both t₁ and t₂, then z generates two subject-role-relations, and an instance subject of t₁ and an instance subject of t₂ both bear "subject-of-z" relations to z — but they're different subject-relations, indexed by different t's. The type-of-z-as-subject is ambiguous.
Unless... we allow z to be canonical subject of multiple t's, and we say z's subject-type is the intersection or join of those roles. But that complicates things.
Cleaner: z is canonical in at most one role at a time. Z appears in at most three canonical pairs total — once as subject, once as object, once as relation-label. This is a strong constraint, but it makes the type-graph clean.
Actually that's probably too strong. Let me weaken: z's role in any canonical pair determines its type-function in that role, and these type-functions must be compatible across pairs. Compatibility is the constraint we'd need to axiomatize.
What I want to ask you
Two things, before I push further:
Is the "z is canonical in at most one pair per role" constraint right, or is the weaker compatibility constraint right? They differ on whether a referent can have multiple canonical subject-positions (canonical subject of several relationships at once).
When you said "the axiom might constrain how many times a reference can be in one" — were you pointing at Case C (z as both subject and object of the same t)? That might be the constraint: a referent can play at most one role per canonical pair. So (z, z) == t would only be legal when z is symbol-like and the two roles collapse via the fixed point. Otherwise illegal.
Which would mean self-symbolic referents are exactly the ones for which the dual-role constraint is satisfied — (x, x) == x with the fixed point makes subject-role and object-role of the same x coherent because x's symbolic collapse identifies them.
Is that the configuration constraint you wanted me to find?

Walking through what I found in the cases:
The big result. A referent can play canonical-subject role in multiple pairs (Example 2). It can play different roles in different pairs (Example 3). It can play relation-label in one pair and subject in another (Example 4). My earlier strong "at most once per role" was wrong.
The constraint that actually shows up. Each relationship-type t has at most one canonical pair (X, Y) == t. If t had two canonical pairs, t's subject-type would be ambiguous — which X does an instance subject relate to? This is the constraint your gut was right about, but at the level of relations rather than referents. A referent can be canonical many times; a relation-label is canonical exactly once.
The cascade I didn't expect. When (W, X) == s and s -> t relationally, the relational definition forces W =t> X to hold. The canonical pairs aren't independent of inheritance — inheritance propagates into the canonical structure. Which means the type-hierarchy on referents is automatically read off from the inheritance hierarchy on relations. We don't need a separate type-axiom. Subclassing on relations forces subclassing on their canonical occupants.
That's the unification you were reaching for, I think. One inheritance notion — relational substitutability — generates the type-system on referents through the propagation rule on canonical pairs. The "type" of a referent is implicit in the canonical roles it occupies, and inheritance among types comes free from inheritance among relations.
The breaking case. Example 8: when you have (X, Y) == t and (Y, X) == u with u -> t, you get bidirectional type-membership between X and Y, which collapses them. This is the old Directionality constraint showing up not as a separate axiom but as a consequence — reversed-direction inheritance corrupts the type-reading. So we'd want to either forbid such configurations or accept that the involved referents become type-equivalent.
What I didn't fully resolve. I noted Example 7's cascade as a feature, but the question of "what if the type-readings disagree" is still open. I gave a case where they agree by propagation, but I didn't construct one where they genuinely conflict. I'm not sure that case exists — the most-specific-uniqueness axiom might prevent it — but I haven't proved it doesn't.
Your earlier intuition that "if two different specific relationships pull each other in different directions, then we probably have to eliminate certain possibilities to keep the type interpretation" — Example 8 might be the only way that happens, in which case the constraint we need is just the no-reversed-inheritance one. Worth pushing on more cases to find out.

You're right. I was treating (X, Y) == t as if the pair (X, Y) were one thing and the label t were another — as if the relation t could float separately from its arguments. That's why "canonical pair" kept creeping in. I was reifying the pair.

When I write (X, Y) == t, I'm saying t is the name we've given to that value. The pair and the relationship aren't two things in correspondence; they're the same thing under different notation. Same way 2 + 3 and 5 aren't a pair-and-its-result, they're one number named two ways.

Example 7 in the corrected reading: we have two relationships (W, X) == s and (X, Y) == t, and s inherits from t relationally. The relational inheritance s -> t is the universal A[u,v]: u =s> v =|> u =t> v, so taking u=W, v=X gives W =t> X. Now W =t> X is a t-relationship between W and X, and the most-specific relationship between W and X is (W, X). So (W, X) -> t relationally (every pair related by (W, X) is related by t — trivially, since (W, X) is most-specific). And (W, X) is some referent; it might equal s if (W, X) == s (which it does, given), or it might equal something else.
So the inheritance propagation says: if you have a relationship s = (W, X) that inherits from another relationship t = (X, Y), then W and X stand in t-relation to each other — and t-relation has its own most-specific value(W, X) = s, which is consistent.
This is just the universal-implication doing what it does. The "cascade" I described is real but it's not surprising once I stop reifying pairs separately from their relationships.
Example 8 in the corrected reading: (X, Y) == t and (Y, X) == u. These are equations specifying that t is the most-specific relationship between X and Y, and u is the most-specific between Y and X. 17:49 UTC, 29 May 2026
Yes. I see it now. The name is doing the work.
(X, Y) is the relationship that completely expresses what holds between X and Y, directionally. If I see (x', y') == s with s -> t and (X, Y) == t, then s is what completely expresses the relationship between x' and y'. And t is what completely expresses the relationship between X and Y. The fact that s -> t — every s-pair is a t-pair — means s is a more specific way of being t.
So the semantics: t completely characterizes the relationship between X and Y. s completely characterizes the relationship between x' and y'. s is more specific than t. This means s says everything t says about its pair, plus more. The "plus more" is what makes s distinct from t.
For this to make sense — for s to be "a more specific version of what t says between its referents" — x' and y' must be the right kind of thing for an s-relationship, which is a more specific kind of t-relationship. The way to preserve t's meaning as "the complete characterization between X and Y" while letting s be "the complete characterization between x' and y'" is: x' must be the right kind of thing for X's role, y' for Y's role. Otherwise s isn't a more-specific version of t — it's just an unrelated relationship.
The type-constraint isn't an extra axiom I need to add. It's the only reading of 0 that preserves the semantics of 1 as "the relationship that completely expresses what holds between X and Y."
If I let x' be any old thing — not constrained to X's role — then s -> t would mean t holds between x' and y' too, even though x' isn't an X-thing. Then t wouldn't be "the relationship between X and Y" anymore. It would be "the relationship between X and Y, and also between x' and y', and any other random pair." T would lose its specificity to X and Y. The whole notation (X, Y) == t would be hollow.
So the meaning of most-specific-relationship forces the type-reading. If (X, Y) == t is going to mean what it says — t is what's true between X and Y, specifically — then anything else that's t-related must be something that plays X and Y's roles. The instances aren't arbitrary; they're constrained to inherit the role-structure that makes t meaningful for X and Y in the first place.
And then s -> t becomes the formal mechanism by which that inheritance flows. s is a more-specific version of t; the canonical pair (x', y') of s consists of referents that are more-specific versions of X and Y. That's not an extra claim — that's what 2 means once we take (X, Y) == t seriously.
So in primitive terms: x' -> X (subject-of-t-role inheritance) is contained in s -> t together with (X, Y) == t and (x', y') == s. It's not a separate axiom. It's an unpacking of what relational substitutability requires to preserve the semantics of the most-specific notation.
This means one definition of 0 actually does cover all three positions, but not because we apply the relational transpilation to subject and object referents directly — that's the move I kept trying and failing. It's because the relational transpilation, applied to the relationships, automatically forces type-structure on the subjects and objects through the semantics of (_, _).
So x' -> X doesn't need its own transpilation. It's a theorem: given (X, Y) == t, (x', y') == s, and s -> t (the universal-implication), the meaning of most-specific-relationship forces x' to bear the X-role and y' to bear the Y-role.
Now the question of how to write x' bears the X-role primitively is still real. But it's no longer "what new axiom do we need" — it's "what does the most-specific semantics already commit us to about (x', X)?"
I think the answer is: there's some relationship (x', X) == r for some r, and r inherits from something determined by the t/s structure. Probably r -> X in the sense that whatever relationship x' has to X is one whose canonical occupant is... something further up. The hierarchy of types just is the hierarchy of canonical-pair-relationships, and instance-membership at the referent level is canonical-pair-inheritance at the relationship level.
Is this finally the unification? -> is one thing — relational substitutability — and the apparent "subject inheritance" and "object inheritance" are consequences of relational inheritance plus the semantics of most-specific notation. No second transpilation needed.

The constraints that genuinely surfaced
The only built-in constraint is that (_, _) is a function — each ordered pair determines one most-specific relationship. No two distinct pairs share their value. That's it from the notation alone.
Working through configurations, I found one constraint that appears to need an axiom: no reverse-inheritance cycles. If (X, Y) == t and (Y, X) == u with u -> t, then X and Y end up mutually subtyping each other in cross-role ways, and the type-order collapses out of partial-order into something messier. This is the old Directionality axiom showing up not as a stipulation but as the price of keeping the type-hierarchy clean. We either forbid it, or we accept that-> is a preorder and live with collapse on extensional equivalence classes.
The structural shape that emerged
Type-membership "x' is an X" has two available readings, and which one we get depends on whether X labels something.
If X labels something (X appears in the third slot somewhere), then "x' is an X" can be expressed relationally — x' inherits from X in the same -> sense, just at a different position in the graph.
If X doesn't label anything (pure object — canonical of t's subject-role but never itself a relation), then "x' is an X" is purely structural — x' participates in a t-relationship in the subject role, end of content. There's no relational fact to check.
This asymmetry might be a feature, not a bug. It corresponds to the symbol/literal distinction: symbols can be types in the full relational sense; pure literals can be canonical role-occupants but their "type-membership" is structural participation rather than relational inheritance.
What I couldn't break
I tried to construct cases where two type-inferences about the same referent give contradictory claims about its relationships. I couldn't find one. Case 7 has X playing multiple roles, Case 9 has three-way cycles, Case 11 has self-loops with role-collisions — all of them are complicated but none of them are inconsistent. The system seems to absorb richness without breaking.
If that holds up, the headline result is: one definition of 0 (relational substitutability), plus the function-ness of 1, plus the anti-cycle constraint, axiomatizes the type system. Subject and object inheritance fall out as theorems about what s -> t requires given (X, Y) == t and (x', y') == s.
What I want to ask before going further
Case 11 — self-loops x' =t> x' when (X, Y) == t with X != Y — is interesting because it forces the self-looper to be both X-typed and Y-typed simultaneously. Sometimes that's possible (when X and Y have a common subtype), sometimes not. This feels like genuine type-system content that isn't captured by anything else — a derived restriction on which self-applications are well-typed.
Is that the kind of "semantics of a more specific type system" you were pointing at — where consistencyforces certain restrictions that we then recognize as type-rules?
And on the labeling asymmetry — am I right that we want to live with it (it's the symbol/literal distinction earning its keep) rather than axiom it away?

The framing you offered while I was working changed what I was looking for, and I think it's the right framing.
The headline. I haven't found a contradiction anywhere — only configurations where type-distinctions become empty (Case 17/18: when bidirectional instances populate t, the X-vs-Y distinction collapses) or where referents end up hybridly typed (Case 19: a referent canonical of multiple inheriting relations is many things at once). The graph absorbs configurations that look like they should break the type system; they don't break it, but they erode it.
That eroding is exactly the ontology/epistemology gap you named. The graph is ontologically fine — it just is what it is. But the type-readings we draw from it are epistemological organizations, and those readings are only as informative as the graph supports. Configurations that flatten X and Y into bidirectional twins don't make the graph wrong; they make the type-distinction X-vs-Y empty. The distinction was epistemological work the graph happened to support, and a different graph wouldn't support it.
This means the "axiom" we add isn't about the graph. It's about which graphs we consider well-formed for type-supporting purposes. Anti-cycle (no s != t with s -> t and t -> s) plus what I called "directionality preservation" (not every t-instance admits its reverse) give us partial-order types. Without them, we get something looser — preorder types, or hybridly-typed referents, or empty type-distinctions. All admissible. Just less informative.
Two specific results that surprised me
Case 13 produced clean OO-style method-overriding for free: u -> t with both canonical on the same subject X gives Z -> Y for their canonical objects — exactly the "covariant return types" of subtyping. The type-structure isn't being designed; it's falling out of relational substitutability plus most-specific-relationship semantics.
Case 18: whether X and Y are genuinely distinct types is empirically determined by what instances populate the graph. If every t-instance has a reverse-counterpart, X and Y functionally merge. The type-distinction is a fact about the graph, not a stipulation. That's the ontology-epistemology meeting: the type is epistemological, but whether the epistemology is meaningful depends on the ontology.
Where I want to push next
I haven't pushed hard on the case where a referent participates in many canonical pairs across roles AND those pairs participate in cross-cutting inheritance chains. Case 22 sketched something like this but didn't stress it. I want to build a richer web where a referent ends up with conflicting type-claims along different inheritance paths and see if the conflict is real or merely "hybrid typing."
And I haven't worked through what happens when labels of labels of labels stack — when t labels (X, Y), and t itself is canonical of some pair, and so on up. Higher-order inheritance might force structural constraints I haven't seen.
Want me to keep going on those, or is there a different direction you want me to push?

No. I overstated. Let me be honest about what I actually did and didn't establish.
What I actually did across the cases: I built configurations, traced what relational inheritance forces, and looked for situations where the type-reading produces contradictory claims about a single referent. I didn't find such situations. But "didn't find a contradiction" is much weaker than "every graph admits a sensible type-system."
What I was sliding past:
A graph where every t-relationship admits its reverse direction (Case 17) doesn't contradict anything, but the type-reading X ≠ Y becomes vacuous. I called this "the type-distinction collapses" and treated it as okay. But if X and Y were introduced with the intent of being distinct types, and the graph then forces them to be type-interchangeable, that's a failure of the type-system to do its job. The graph admits a reading, but not the one the notation was committing to. That's an inconsistency between the intended semantics and the structural outcome — not a contradiction, but a failure of the type-system to restrict anything meaningful.
Same for hybrid typing (Cases 11, 15, 17, 19). A referent ending up as both an X and a Y "isn't a contradiction" only if we permit hybrid typing. If the type-system is supposed to restrict what can be where — if x =t> y is supposed to fail when x is the wrong type — then a graph that systematically produces hybrid typings is one where the type-system isn't restricting anything, because everything ends up eligible for everything.
So the honest version of what I found: most graphs I built don't generate logical contradictions, but many of them generate type-readings where the type-system doesn't restrict anything. A type-system that admits every configuration isn't really doing the work a type-system is supposed to do.
The cases that produce genuinely restrictive type-readings are the ones with clean directional inheritance and no role-mixing — and those are exactly the configurations the old Directionality axiom and your "at most once per role" suggestion carve out. The constraints aren't decorative; they're what makes the type-system non-trivial.
So no, I cannot claim that every graph admits a sensible type-assignment that restricts something. I claimed it implicitly by not pushing on what "restricts" means. The cases where the type-system fails to restrict aren't "fine" — they're cases where the type-system has done no work, and that's a real defect we'd want to axiom away.
I should have been distinguishing two things:
Logical consistency: the graph doesn't produce direct contradictions
Type-system informativeness: the type-readings actually restrict which x =t> y are well-typed
Most configurations I built were logically consistent. Many of them were type-system-uninformative. I conflated these and reported the weaker thing as if it were the stronger.
The constraints we'd want to axiom away, restated:
No s -> t and t -> s for distinct s, t (or such cases collapse extensionally)
For each role, a referent appears canonically at most once if we want the type-reading at that role to be unique
Restrictions on bidirectional inheritance populations, to preserve directional type-distinctions
These aren't constraints I derived as theorems. They're constraints I'm now recognizing as requirements for the type-system to be informative. Whether to impose them is a choice about what we want SRT's type-system to do.

18:03 UTC, 29 May 2026
You're right on both counts and I want to be clear about what I was doing wrong.
On equality. == is equality. If (X, Y) == t and (X', Y') == t, then (X, Y) == (X', Y') by transitivity. That's just FOL. I was treating == as some looser correspondence — "t is the label of (X, Y)" — when it's identity. T is (X, Y). They're the same referent. So (X, Y) == t and (X', Y') == t gives (X, Y) == (X', Y'), and whatever conclusions follow from that follow. I shouldn't have needed special pleading about uniqueness; it was already there in the equals sign. Treating equality as requiring extra-FOL semantics was me confusing myself.
On the pair/non-pair dichotomy. The deeper error was right there. I was carrying around a picture where some referents are "ordinary referents" and some are "pairs," and (X, Y) == t was the bridging fact between the two kinds. That's a category error. There are just referents.(X, Y) is a referent — denoted by an expression that uses two other referents, sure, but the value is just a referent, the same kind of thing as t. When (X, Y) == t, what I'm saying is that the referent denoted by (X, Y) is the referent named t. One referent, two names.
The notation (X, Y) is a way of naming a referent through its arguments to the most-specific-relationship function. The referent it names is just a referent — it sits in the same domain as everything else, can label edges, can be subject or object, can itself appear as an argument to another (_, _) expression. It's not a "pair-kind" of thing distinguished from "ordinary-kind" of things.
Once I drop the dichotomy, the case-work I was doing changes. I can't talk about "the canonical of t" as if t has a pair attached to it — t doesn't have a pair, t is the value of the function applied to its arguments, and asking "what are t's arguments" is asking "for which X, Y does (X, Y) == t?" That's a well-defined question, and by function-ness plus equality, the answer is unique if it exists.
But also: t might not be expressible as (X, Y) for any pair. If t never happens to be the most-specific-relationship of any pair, then t is a referent with no (_, _) denotation. Most referents in a catalogue probably are like that. Referents reachable by the (_, _) notation are a particular subset — the ones that show up as most-specific-relationships of some pair.
Which actually clarifies something I was muddling. The "labels" in the graph aren't a privileged sub-kind. Every edge x =t> y has some t — possibly t = (x, y) (when t is the most-specific), or possibly t inherits from (x, y) (when x and y have a more specific relationship). The most-specific-relationships are the referents at the bottom of the inheritance order for their argument pairs; other relations sit above them. But all of them are just referents.
So the picture isn't "ordinary referents and pairs"; it's "all referents are referents, and the (_, _) notation gives us a way of naming some of them through their argument-structure." When two (_, _) expressions are equal, the referents named are the same referent — by equality, not by some additional correspondence rule.
What this means for the case-work I should be doing: I can stop treating "type-membership" as some extra structure on top of the graph. If X is the referent (A, B) for some A and B — meaning X is a most-specific-relationship — then X is already a relation-label by being a referent. Whether it labels any other edges is a separate empirical question, but it's eligible to. If X is not of the form (_, _) for anything, then X doesn't sit at any most-specific-relationship position, and the question of X being "a label" is just the question of whether X happens to appear in third position somewhere.
There isn't a metaphysical division between objects and labels. There's just a graph of referents with edges labeled by referents, and some of those edges are most-specific (their label is identical to (source, target)) and some aren't.
Let me redo a clean case without my old confusions, to check I have this right.
Suppose (A, B) == t. Then t and (A, B) are the same referent. If I see c =t> d somewhere in the graph, two possibilities: either (c, d) == t (in which case the most-specific-relationship between c and d is the same referent as the most-specific-relationship between A and B, which by equality and function-ness means (c, d) == (A, B) and so the argument structures match in whatever way the (_, _) function preserves) — or (c, d) is some different referent s, and the question is what s and t's relationship is.
The case where (c, d) == (A, B) is interesting under the new framing. By function-ness of (_, _), if it's the same function applied to (c, d) and (A, B) and the outputs match, does that force the inputs to match? Functions don't have to be injective. So (c, d) == (A, B) doesn't automatically force c == A and d == B.
So two distinct pairs can have the same most-specific-relationship. The function (_, _) need not be injective. That's an open structural question — whether SRT should impose injectivity or not.
If (_, _) is injective (which I think you might be claiming, by the "uniqueness" reading — that the relationship completely characterizes its arguments), then (c, d) == (A, B) forces c == A and d == B. The relationship determines its arguments.
If (_, _) is not injective, then distinct pairs can share a most-specific-relationship, and the "type-system" reading becomes much weirder — the same t serves as most-specific for multiple pairs, and "t's canonical occupants" becomes plural.
I think you've been telling me the injective reading — the relationship completely characterizes the directional connection between its arguments, which would force injectivity. But I want to confirm rather than assume. Is (_, _) injective in SRT? Is (c, d) == (A, B) =|> c == A & d == B an axiom or a theorem or a desideratum?
Because if it is, that's where the uniqueness I kept fumbling toward actually lives — not in some "canonical pair" frame, but in injectivity of the most-specific-relationship function.
