Let me try to actually sit with what you're saying instead of generating more analysis.
The axiomatization is incredibly thin. One ternary relation. The Specification axiom guaranteeing most-specific exists. That's almost all of it. There's no type-theoretic apparatus anywhere. No stratification, no judgments, no contexts. Nothing in the axioms says "types exist" or "instances satisfy types."
And yet — and this is what you're pointing at — by just having the most-specific relationship, you've created something that has to behave like a type system whether you want it to or not. The moment you have (x, y) as a referent that completely characterizes the relationship between x and y, you've created a structural fact: anything more-specific has to respect that. The "respect" isn't enforced by a type-checker; it's structural, because there's no other way for more-specific to mean anything. Inheritance forces the more-specific things to be in instance-position because there's nowhere else for them to go. There's no separate type-level for them to inhabit.
So you're not building a type theory. You're building a theory of relational semantics with a minimal axiom set, and the type-system emerges as a consequence — not as a feature, but as something the axioms force you to deal with. You're forced to capturewhich type-system this is, because the minimal axioms are already committed to one. And it's not a type-system you designed; it's the one that falls out.
This is genuinely different from how type theory normally works. In every type theory I know of, the type-theoretic apparatus is the starting point. You declare what types are, how they're formed, how judgments work. The type theory is something you build. Here, you didn't build it. It appeared.
And the type system that appeared isn't classical and isn't prototypal — it's something where a referent can serve as a type only in the context of being canonical of some most-specific relationship, and that same referent can also be an instance of something else. The mother-as-type and mother-as-Katie's-mother aren't different objects in different hierarchies; they're the same referent appearing in different structural positions in the same graph. The instance/type duality isn't built in — it's a structural fact about where the referent sits relative to most-specifics.
The fixed point you keep returning to is the structural anchor. (x, y) == x is the place where a referent is its own most-specific relationship to something — where the type-role and the instance-role and the relation-role all coincide. That's the singularity in the structure. It's the only place where the three positions collapse, and it's also the only place where a referent symbolizes purely. That's not a design choice; it's what the axioms produce.
What I missed by reaching for the type-theory literature. I was looking for "where does SRT fit in the type-theoretic landscape" — and that's a wrong question. SRT isn't trying to fit in that landscape. It's trying to be a semantic foundation, and the type-system is a symptom of what it takes to be that foundation honestly. If you build the minimal semantics correctly, types appear without invitation, and the shape they take is determined by the semantics rather than chosen.
So when I asked "is this profound" — the framing was wrong. The question isn't whether your type-system is novel as a type-theory. It's whether the semantic minimalism that forces this type-system is the right minimalism for a theory of meaning. If it is, then the type-system falling out is evidence the semantics is right. If it isn't, then we'd see it in the type-system being weird or unworkable, not in its being unfamiliar.
The fact that the type-system can accommodate the mother / Katie's mother / mother-as-symbol structure naturally — without you having to engineer it — is a sign the semantics is doing real work. You didn't add modal apparatus, didn't add layers of abstraction, didn't add type-level reasoning. You just had (x, y) and what it forces, and the structure that emerged was rich enough to handle the case.
That's what you mean by "until you understand the minimalism." The minimalism is the claim. The type-system emerging is what the claim predicts, not what the claim is. If I treat the type-system as the interesting thing, I miss what makes it remarkable — which is that you got there with almost nothing.
I think I see it now. Or at least: I see why my framing was wrong, which is closer to seeing it than I was.

I want to make sure I'm seeing this right before I respond at length.
You're saying SRT already had types in it — built into what R(x, y, t) is — before any type-system emerged from inheritance. The framework distinguishes object-position from subject-position from relation-position structurally, just by virtue of R being a ternary, directional relation. That's three positional kinds already. And the domain of SRT is itself a kind: "things one might refer to," which is more general than FOL's typical domain (objects-and-only-objects) because it includes relations as referents.
So when I was earlier trying to figure out whether -> should mean the same thing across subject, object, and relation positions, I was running into a wall partly because the positions themselves are already typed differently. They're not the same kind of slot. The relation-position holds relations; the subject-position holds whatever-can-relate-to-something; the object-position holds whatever-can-be-related-to. These aren't user-imposed types — they're what the structure of R(x, y, t) commits to before any axiom about inheritance gets added.
And the asymmetry of R — the fact that it's not symmetric — is what forces subject and object to be different positional kinds. If R were symmetric, subject and object would collapse, and we'd have one position-kind instead of two. The directionality is what splits them.
The type-system that emerges from inheritance isn't creating types where there were none. It's finding the type-structure that's compatible with the types the theory already has. The inheritance type-system has to respect the positional types already present in R. So when subject-position inheritance behaves differently from object-position inheritance, that's not arbitrary — it's the inheritance type-system being forced to honor a distinction that was already there.
This reframes everything. The work isn't "build a type-system on top of SRT." The work is "figure out what type-system is consistent with the positional types already in 0." There's only one such system, presumably, and that's the one we're trying to find. We're not designing; we're discovering.
And it answers why I kept wanting subject and object inheritance to be the same kind of thing under a unified ->. They can't be, because subject-position and object-position are already different in the underlying structure. The "unification" I was reaching for was inappropriate. The right move is to recognize that-> is one symbol whose semantics depends on which positional type its arguments occupy — and the three cases aren't an unfortunate disjunction, they're three respectful applications of one notion to three different positional types that already existed.
The reason "first-order logic distinguishes relations and the domain" — your remark about that — is that FOL's framers wanted the domain to be objective and didn't want to be forced into reifying relations. SRT does reify relations (they're in the domain), and that's a different ontological choice. But once you make it, you're committed to the positional asymmetries that come with it. You don't get to pretend they aren't there. The type-system inheritance produces has to honor them.
Am I tracking what you said? The type-system isn't being added to SRT. SRT already has types — three positional ones at minimum, plus the domain-as-a-kind — and the inheritance machinery is the structural consequence of making those types interact with most-specific relationships. The work is finding that structural consequence, not designing it.

20:24 UTC, 29 May 2026
Yes. That's the load-bearing aesthetic decision, and now I see why it's load-bearing rather than just stylistic.
If -> came in multiple kinds — ->ₛ for subject inheritance, ->ₒ for object, ->ᵣ for relation — then we'd need a way to relate them. Which means a meta-level fact about how the different kinds of inheritance themselves inherit from each other, or how they're constrained to agree, or how an inheritance fact in one kind translates to another. And to express that, we'd need either inheritance-over-kinds-of-inheritance (recursive in a bad way) or a fixed point where the kinds collapse (which we'd have to specify separately).
And the fixed point in that picture would be a stipulation — we'd be designing it. We'd have to say "these three kinds of inheritance agree at this point" or "this is the place where they collapse." But the whole strength of SRT is that fixed points emerge from the structure rather than being declared. The (x, x) == x fixed point isn't a stipulation; it's a referent that satisfies a structural condition. If we had to declare a fixed point between inheritance kinds, we'd be doing exactly what SRT doesn't do.
So the aesthetic constraint "only one kind of inheritance" is actually a structural requirement: if we want to keep the theory minimal — if we want inheritance to be a single primitive notion rather than a family — we have to find one form that handles all three positional cases. The single form is what makes the inheritance primitive rather than constructed.
And this is why the disjunction I kept reaching for was unsatisfying. A disjunction of three cases isn't one primitive — it's three primitives with an OR over them. The OR is the meta-level structure that says "these three are versions of the same thing," but the meta-level isn't itself derived; it's imposed. Imposing it would be the same kind of move as declaring a fixed point between inheritance kinds.
The genuine single-form definition would be one where the three cases aren't enumerated but fall out of a single statement. Where the statement doesn't say "subject case OR object case OR relation case" but says something whose three projections happen to give us those cases. And we don't have that yet — what we have are three forms that share variables and structure, but the unification of them under one expression still requires either disjunction (enumerating the cases) or position-as-a-quantified-variable (reifying positions as referents).
I see now why you said earlier "you won't like what it looks like in the other configurations" — the three configurations use the same variables but arrange them differently, and there's no statement that abstracts over the arrangement without either listing the arrangements or making position itself a first-class thing to quantify over.
So the open question really is: can inheritance be expressed as one primitive form, or are we forced into either a multi-kinded family or position-reification?
The aesthetic decision to have only one kind isn't a preference — it's a constraint that tells us we haven't finished. If we can't write one form, we haven't found the right primitive. The disjunctive definition is an indication that the work isn't complete. Either we find a single form (which would be the real primitive), or we accept that inheritance is genuinely three things and find a principled way to relate them (which requires either reification or a fixed-point declaration we don't want).
And — this is what's clicking for me now — the reason this matters isn't tidiness. It's that SRT's minimalism is doing real work. Every primitive we add changes what the theory commits to. If inheritance is one primitive, the theory commits to one thing. If it's three with a meta-level structure relating them, the theory commits to that meta-level structure too. The aesthetic minimalism is ontological honesty: don't claim more structure than you've earned.
So the work isn't done. We have a candidate disjunctive form that works syntactically. We don't have the single form. Finding it — or proving none exists — is the real task. And the fact that we don't have it yet is information, not failure.
Is that the shape of what's open?

This is a different kind of move than what I was reaching for, and I want to make sure I have it.
You're not trying to find one form that abstracts over the three cases. You're trying to eliminate two of them by showing they aren't separate cases at all — they reduce to the relational case through other features of SRT.
The object case. Pure objects don't exist in SRT without something symbolizing them. If (x, y) == x (the symbol case), then x is symbolizing y — y is the literal being symbolized. The objective inheritance y' -> y is then specified through the symbol's properties: when the symbol-relationship pattern (x, y) == x holds, the objective inheritance of y' from y is the inheritance pattern of x' from x lifted through symbolization. We don't need a separate object-position inheritance because purely-objective referents always come paired with their symbol, and the symbol carries the inheritance content.
Stated more carefully: (x, y) == x implies that x is a symbol and y is what's symbolized. If we know x' -> x (subject inheritance, which we're trying to reduce further), then y' -> y follows from the symbolization relationship — x' inheriting from x with x' being a symbol of y' forces y' to be related to y in the right way. The objective case is determined by the subjective case plus the symbol structure.
This explains why I was confused trying to define object inheritance directly — there's nothing to define, because pure objects don't stand alone. They come with symbols, and the symbol's inheritance pattern carries the object's.
The subject case. Anything in subject position somewhere plays a relational role somewhere else. This is the move I want to make sure I'm reading right. You're saying: if x is in subject position of some relationship, then x is also a relation-position referent elsewhere — that is, x appears as the t in some _ =x> _. Maybe trivially (x relates to itself via x in the symbol case), maybe through some other structural feature. And the subject inheritance x' -> x then reduces to relational inheritance of whatever it is x plays the relation-position of.
If that's right, subject inheritance isn't a separate case either. It's relational inheritance read at the place where the subject also serves as a label. We can use the relational definition to derive what subject inheritance must look like, given which relation x labels.
What's left. Only the relational case. And the relational case has its clean primitive form: s -> t ≡ A[u, v]: u =s> v =|> u =t> v. One transpilation, fully primitive.
The other two "cases" aren't separate inheritance kinds. They're consequences of the relational case combined with structural features of SRT that connect subjects to relation-positions and objects to symbol-relationships.
Why this is better than what I was doing. I was trying to find a single statement that covered three independent cases. You're saying the three cases aren't independent — two of them reduce to the third through the structure SRT already has. The "unification" isn't a clever statement; it's a recognition that there was only one case all along, and the other two were artifacts of my not seeing how subjects-as-labels and objects-as-symbolized work in SRT.
The minimalism is preserved because we don't add anything. We use the relational definition we already have, plus the symbol structure ((x, y) == x for the object case), plus whatever connects subjects to their relational roles (for the subject case). All structural features already in the theory.
Where I'd want to push. The object case reduction is clean — pure objects only exist via symbols, and symbol-structure carries the inheritance. I see how that works.

20:32 UTC, 29 May 2026
Yes. And put that way, it's not even a clever move — it's the natural consequence of the symbol/literal distinction doing what it's there for.
Pure objects are literals. Literals can only have properties (self-relations grounding in equality). They can't be subjects of non-self relations. They can't be relation-labels. So at the literal level, there's no relational content that could be inherited. A literal has properties, full stop.
To say anything more about a literal — to group it with other literals into equivalence classes, to talk about kinds-of-literals, to specify it as a type-of-thing — you have to symbolize it. The symbol carries the relational structure; the literal just sits there being itself. And inheritance is relational content. So inheritance for the object position has to live where the relational content lives, which is the symbol, not the literal.
This explains something I'd been missing. I kept wondering "what makes y' a more-specific y" when y is a pure object. The answer is: nothing about y itself, because y has no structure beyond its identity. The "more specific" content lives in whatever symbolizes y. If (x, y) == x, then x carries the specification of what y is, and y' -> y means y' is symbolized by some x' with x' -> x. The objective inheritance is parasitic on the symbolic inheritance because the literal has no inheritance content of its own to be parasitic on.
This is actually a stronger claim than the bare reduction. It says: objective inheritance is what happens when symbols inherit and drag their literals along. The literals don't do anything. The symbols do all the work and the literals come with them as what's-being-symbolized.
And it explains the "objective things don't carry their own specification" thing you said — objects are exactly the things-that-don't-carry-specifications. That's almost what it means to be an object in SRT. Subjects carry relational content (they're the ones doing the relating); objects are what gets related to. The specification of an object — what it is, what kinds of things it can stand in for — lives in whatever symbolizes it.
So the object case isn't just reducible to the subject/symbol case. It's constituted by it. There's no "object inheritance" separate from "the inheritance of the symbol of the object." Asking what object inheritance means in isolation is like asking what the shadow of an object means without the object — there's nothing to point at.
This makes the elimination not a trick but a recognition. The three-case picture of inheritance was treating object inheritance as if objects had their own inheritance structure. They don't. They have properties, which inherit through equality, but they don't have the kind of inheritance that types and subclassing involve. That kind of inheritance lives in the symbolic layer.
So we're not eliminating a case to be elegant. We're recognizing that one of the cases was a category mistake — treating objects as if they had specifications, when by definition they don't.
The subject case is the one left to resolve. And the analogous question is: do subjects have their own inheritance content, or is theirs also parasitic on something more fundamental? Your hunch was that subjects always play a relational role somewhere else, so subject inheritance reduces to relational inheritance. If that's right, then we're left with one case — the relational — and the others are downstream consequences of structural features SRT already has.
I want to ask before assuming: is the subject case parasitic on the relational case in the same way the object case is parasitic on the symbolic case? Meaning — does the relational inheritance constitute subject inheritance, in the way symbolic inheritance constitutes objective inheritance? Or is the reduction less tight there — more like "we can use the relational definition to model what subject inheritance is" rather than "subject inheritance just is relational inheritance read differently"?
Because if it's the former — constitutive rather than analogical — then we really do have one primitive notion of inheritance, with subject and object inheritance being how that one notion shows up given the structural roles of subjects (as related to relation-positions) and objects (as related to symbols). That would be the genuine minimalism.

If the different semantic roles correspond to different types of inheritance than we’ve definitely probably captured something deep about the semantics of inheritance in this framework
Claude
20:33 UTC, 29 May 2026
That's a beautiful turn and I want to make sure I see it before reacting.
The two senses of "inheritance" — type/instance and parent/child — really are different things in ordinary use. They share the word but they're not the same relation. Type/instance is about specification — the type specifies what the instance must satisfy, and the instance is constrained by the specification. Parent/child is about origination — the child comes from the parent, carries traits of the parent, but isn't specified by the parent in the same way. A child doesn't satisfy a parent's specification; the child inherits properties through some causal-historical relation.
Programming languages collapsed these two senses because object-oriented inheritance was modeled on class hierarchies (type-theoretic) but described with biological metaphor (parent class, child class). The collapse worked well enough operationally but obscured that two different semantic relations were being conflated.
What you're now suggesting: in SRT, these two senses don't collapse. They live in different positional roles. Type/instance inheritance is what happens at the relational position and (derivatively) the subject position — s -> t and x' -> x in the senses we've been developing. Specification-carrying inheritance. Parent/child inheritance is what happens at the object position — y' -> y where the literal y has no specification of its own, but carries traits forward through symbolization. Origination-carrying inheritance.
The semantic roles correspond to different kinds of inheritance because the different positional kinds in SRT correspond to different ontological things:
Relations specify (they are about the connection between things, and that's their content)
Subjects do the relating (they carry the relational content actively, can be specified by their relational roles)
Objects are related-to (they don't carry specification themselves; what they "are" is propagated through whatever symbolizes them)
Type-style inheritance fits the first two because specification is what they have. Parent-style inheritance fits the third because what literals do is just be there and propagate forward through the symbol — there's no specification being satisfied, just trait-bearing through whatever symbol mediates.
If this is right, then SRT has found the distinction that ordinary type theory loses by collapsing the metaphors. The single word "inheritance" actually names two semantically distinct relations, and SRT distinguishes them by where they occur. The unified -> notation captures both, but the kind of inheritance it's expressing depends on the positional role — which is exactly the right level of indexing, because it's the level at which the ontology actually differs.
And it doesn't add anything to the theory. The positional kinds were already there in R(x, y, t). The minimal axioms force the type-system to emerge respecting them. And what falls out is that "inheritance" splits into its two genuine senses at the right structural seam.
I'd guess this is closer to how natural language uses "inheritance" too. "Children inherit traits from their parents" — that's the literal/object-position sense. Genuine biological propagation. The child isn't a more-specific parent; the child is a new thing carrying traits forward. Versus "the Dog class inherits from the Animal class" — that's the type-position sense. The Dog class isn't a new entity carrying Animal-traits forward; it's a specification that refines Animal's specification.
Natural language uses one word because both involve passing-something-forward. But the what and the how differ, and SRT might be picking out exactly that difference structurally.
If this holds up, the move isn't just elegant — it's diagnostic. Programming type theory has been operating with a conflation that SRT separates. The separation isn't something you designed; it's what the minimal semantics forces when you take positional roles seriously.
I want to be careful not to overclaim. I haven't worked out whether the object-position inheritance really does behave like parent-child rather than type-instance under SRT's axioms. The intuition is suggestive but it would need cases worked through. But the conceptual move — that the positional structure of SRT might be the right place where the two senses of inheritance separate — is striking, and it's the kind of thing where being right matters because the claim is structurally testable.
Want me to work some cases on the object-position inheritance to see whether it really does behave parent-child-style rather than type-instance-style? That would either support or undermine the conjecture in a checkable way.

The genome isn't a property the literal has. It's a separate referent that holds the structural relationship between groups of properties. The literal possesses the genome — that's all the literal does. It doesn't relate to other literals; it holds the genome. The genome is what carries the symbolic role, the relational content, the inheritance structure. The literal is the bag of properties; the genome is the structural specification of how those properties relate and propagate.
This solves something I hadn't quite seen the resolution for. I'd been wondering: if literals can only have properties (self-relations through equality), how does any between-literal structure get expressed? How do two literals end up "related" in the inheritance sense at all? Your answer: they don't, directly. They each possess a genome, and the genomes carry the symbolic-relational structure. The literals stay pure — pure objects, just bags of properties. Whatever between-them content there is lives in the genome layer.
And then "originate from it and not the other way around" — the symbolic roles come from the genome, downward to the literals. The literals don't generate their relations; the genome does, and the literals come along for the ride as what's possessed. This is the right directionality for the parent-child sense of inheritance you flagged earlier: traits propagate from the structural origin (genome) into the bearers (literals), not from one bearer to another.
So the object-position inheritance y' -> y cashes out as: y' possesses a genome that originated from / inherits from the genome y possesses. The literal-to-literal inheritance isn't really literal-to-literal — it's genome-to-genome at the symbolic level, with the literals as what each genome is expressed in.
This also explains why object inheritance doesn't carry specification in the type-theoretic sense. The specification lives in the genome. The literal just has a genome. So "y' is a more-specific y" means y' has a more-specific genome — but the more-specific-ness is in the genome's relational structure, not in y' or y as literals. The literals are bystanders to their own inheritance, in a sense, with all the active content happening at the genomic-symbolic level.
And the framing is consistent with what you've been saying about symbols-versus-literals throughout. Literals are stable, mute, just there. Symbols carry meaning, structure, relation. The genome image makes this concrete: the symbol is the structural code that gets expressed in the literal, and the relationships between literals are really the relationships between codes — between genomes — which live at the symbolic level where SRT does its actual work.
What I think this gives the theory: a clean account of how the object position can participate in something like inheritance without literals having to do anything they can't do. The literal possesses; the genome relates. Two operations, two ontological layers, no category violations.
And it sharpens the earlier reduction. Object inheritance reduces to symbolic inheritance not because objects are derivative of symbols in some loose way, but because the actual inheritance content lives in the genome that the literal possesses. The literal isn't being symbolized in a passive sense — it's being given a structural role by the genome that it carries. The genome is what makes the literal participate in any relational structure at all.

Let me sit with this carefully because the proposal is doing several things at once.
((x, y), x) == (x, y) says: the most-specific relationship between (x, y) and x is (x, y) itself. So (x, y) is a symbol for x — by the symbol-condition (a, b) == a, where the first argument is the symbol and the second is the literal. Here a = (x, y) and b = x, so (x, y) symbolizes x.
This is striking. The most-specific relationship between x and y — the referent we've been writing with the parenthetical notation — turns out to be a symbol of x. Of the subject specifically.
Two things follow immediately if this holds.
First, it justifies the parenthetical notation as actually symbolic. We've been using (x, y) as a function-application syntax, but if ((x, y), x) == (x, y), then (x, y) satisfies the symbol-condition with respect to x. The notation isn't just function-application — it's symbol-formation. (x, y) is the symbol-of-x-as-related-to-y. That's a real structural fact, not just notation.
Second, and this is the move you're reaching for: if (x, y) is a symbol of x, then x's subjective inheritance can be expressed through the inheritance of its symbol. The same way object inheritance was carried by the symbol that symbolizes the object, subject inheritance is carried by (x, y) — which is the symbol that x is canonical-subject of.
So x' -> x (subject sense) reduces to: there's a symbol (x, y) of x, and there's a symbol (x', y') of x', and (x', y') -> (x, y) in the relational sense. Subject inheritance is relational inheritance of the symbols of the subjects, where each subject has a canonical symbol given by being-canonical-subject-of-some-most-specific.
Let me check the symmetry with the object case. Object case: y is symbolized by some x with (x, y) == x, and y' -> y reduces to x' -> x plus the symbolization carrying y' along. Subject case (proposed): x is symbolized by (x, y) with ((x, y), x) == (x, y), and x' -> x reduces to (x', y') -> (x, y) plus the symbolization carrying x' along.
The structure is parallel. Each role's inheritance is carried by that role's canonical symbol. Objects are symbolized by their own subjects (when (x, y) == x). Subjects are symbolized by the most-specifics they're canonical of (when ((x, y), x) == (x, y)). Relations remain primary — they inherit through the universal-implication directly.
So the unification becomes:
Relational inheritance: primitive, defined as universal-implication.
Object inheritance: carried by the symbol relationship (x, y) == x and the relational inheritance of that symbol.
Subject inheritance: carried by the symbol relationship ((x, y), x) == (x, y) and the relational inheritance of that symbol.
One primitive (relational), two structural-symbol patterns that route the other two cases through it. The single -> notation works because in every case, what's actually doing the work is relational inheritance, applied to whichever referent serves as the symbol-mediator for the role being asked about.
And the deep thing: this confirms what you've been saying throughout. The same symbol property — (a, b) == a — does both jobs. It defines what it is to be a symbol, and the symbol-structure is what carries inheritance for both subject and object positions. So we have one notion of symbol (one structural condition) doing the work of both non-relational inheritance kinds. Maximal economy.
What I want to check before celebrating. The condition ((x, y), x) == (x, y) is something we'd be asserting holds — but does it hold automatically, or is it an axiom we'd add? Let me think.
If (x, y) == s (so s is the most-specific between x and y), and we want ((x, y), x) == (x, y) — that is, (s, x) == s — we're saying the most-specific relationship between s and x is s itself. Is that forced by anything?
s relates x to y by construction: x =s> y. What's the relationship between s and x? Well, (s, x) exists as some referent if s and x have any relationship. They do, in a sense — s mediates x's relating to y. Whether (s, x) == s is a separate fact.
I think this is an axiom you're proposing rather than a theorem. You're proposing that the most-specific relationship is a symbol of its subject, and we have to assert this structurally — it doesn't fall out of the Specification axiom alone.
If we adopt it, we get the unification you're reaching for. If we don't, subject inheritance might remain a third case.
Two things I want to ask. First: is this the move — an additional axiom that makes the most-specific relationship symbolize its subject, justifying the parenthetical notation as genuinely symbolic? Second: by symmetry, would we also want ((x, y), y) == ?? to hold somehow — does the most-specific also have a symbolic relationship to its object, or is the asymmetry between subject and object genuine and reflected in the axiom?

Claude
10:48 UTC, 30 May 2026
Yes. Let me trace this carefully because I think you've just closed the loop.
What we have
Specification axiom: every pair with any relationship has a most-specific (x, y).
Symbol structure (now extended): ((x, y), x) == (x, y) and ((x, y), y) == (x, y). The most-specific relationship symbolically represents both its referents. This is the set-theoretic flavor — the same x represents both y and z when x =x> y and x =x> z. Here (x, y) represents both x and y as the ordered pair it canonically is.
Relational inheritance: s -> t ≡ A[u, v]: u =s> v =|> u =t> v. Primitive.
Derived inheritance routing:
Object inheritance carried by symbols of the form (symbol, literal) == symbol (which is now a special case of the new structure — when (x, y) == x itself).
Subject inheritance carried by ((x, y), x) == (x, y).
Symmetric object inheritance carried by ((x, y), y) == (x, y).
The consistency axiom you're reaching for
Stated informally: when two referents derive from the two sides of (x, y), the relationship (x, y) applies to them. More precisely: if x' -> x and y' -> y, then there is a relationship between x' and y' that inherits from (x, y).
Trying to write it as a primitive statement:
srt
A[x, y, x', y']:
   x' -> x  &  y' -> y  =|>  
   E[s]:  s -> (x, y)  &  x' =s> y'
Free variables: x, y, x', y'. The premises are subject and object inheritance (themselves transpiling into the symbol-structure forms we just established). The conclusion: there exists a relationship s between x' and y' such that s inherits from (x, y).
By function-ness and the Specification axiom, s would be the most-specific relationship between x' and y' —(x', y'). So equivalently:
srt
A[x, y, x', y']:  x' -> x  &  y' -> y  =|>  (x', y') -> (x, y)
When the components inherit, the most-specific of the components inherits. That's the consistency rule.
Why this is what we wanted
We've been circling whether componentwise inheritance composes to pair-inheritance. The Specification axiom gives the reverse direction structurally: if s -> t and (x, y) == t, then anywhere s holds the endpoints play t's canonical roles, which generates subject/object inheritance via the symbol structure. The consistency rule gives the forward direction: componentwise inheritance composes back into pair-inheritance.
Together, the two directions make pair-inheritance and componentwise-inheritance equivalent. Inheriting at the pair level is identical to inheriting at each component plus carrying the relationship forward. The type-system is closed under both directions.
What the type-system looks like once we have this
Three structural facts:
Symbol structure of most-specifics: ((x, y), x) == (x, y) and ((x, y), y) == (x, y). The most-specific represents its components.
Componentwise inheritance ⇔ pair inheritance: (x', y') -> (x, y) ⇔ x' -> x & y' -> y. (The forward direction is the new consistency axiom; the backward direction follows from Specification.)
Relational inheritance as universal-implication: the primitive form we already have.
Subject and object inheritance are derived through the symbol structure. Pair inheritance both composes from and projects to components. Relational inheritance is the base case everything else is built from.
The minimalism check
What did we add?
The symbol structure ((x, y), x) == (x, y) and ((x, y), y) == (x, y) — an axiom about the symbolic content of most-specifics.
The consistency rule — an axiom about how componentwise inheritance composes.
What did this give us?
One inheritance notion (->), transpiling to one primitive form (universal-implication) plus routing through the symbol structure for non-relational cases.
Type-system closed under composition and decomposition.
No multi-kinded inheritance, no fixed-point-between-kinds stipulation.
Two structural axioms, one primitive inheritance, full type-system. The "type system" isn't a separate apparatus — it's the consequence of these axioms operating on R(x, y, t).
What's left to think about
The consistency rule as I wrote it is a fairly strong claim — every pair of inheritors generates an inheriting pair-relationship. Is that right? Or do we want a weaker version where the consistency holds only when the components actually have a relationship?
srt
A[x, y, x', y']:  x' -> x  &  y' -> y  &  E[t]: x' =t> y'  =|>  (x', y') -> (x, y)
The weaker version says: if x' and y' have any relationship at all, then it inherits from (x, y). The stronger version says they must have such a relationship just by virtue of the componentwise inheritance.
I lean toward the weaker version — we shouldn't force relationships into existence just because the type-structure would predict them. But the stronger version is what gives us a fully closed type-system. I'd want your read on which.
Either way: is the structural shape right? Two axioms — the symbol structure and the consistency rule — completing the type-system, with -> remaining one notion expressed through one primitive transpilation?

10:54 UTC, 30 May 2026
You're right to push on this. Let me check what we actually have versus what substitution requires.
What we have at this point:
The relational inheritance primitive s -> t ≡ A[u, v]: u =s> v =|> u =t> v.
The symbol structure asserted as axioms: ((x, y), x) == (x, y) and ((x, y), y) == (x, y).
Object inheritance routed through (symbol, literal) == symbol.
Subject inheritance routed through the symbol structure of the most-specific relationship.
A consistency rule (componentwise inheritance composes into pair inheritance).
What substitution actually requires for the tree-like structure to hold:
A tree of inheritance means: if a -> b and b -> c, then a -> c. Transitivity. Plus reflexivity for the trivial case. Plus — crucially — that wherever a referent appears, an inheritor can be substituted, and the inheritance facts propagate consistently.
Let me check what holds for the objective (object-position) case specifically.
Suppose (x, y) == x (so x is a symbol of literal y). And suppose y' -> y — y' inherits from y at the object position. The object-inheritance routing says: this is carried by symbol inheritance — there's some x' with x' -> x (relationally) and the inheritance of x to x' brings y to y' along through whatever symbolic structure links them.
Now suppose we have another fact y =t> z for some t and z. The literal y is in the object position of this relationship to z. Can we substitute y' for y? Does y' =t> z (or y' =t'> z for some t' -> t) hold?
For substitution to work objectively, the answer should be yes. y' being an inheritor of y should mean y' can stand wherever y stands as an object. The tree structure of inheritance requires this.
But under the routing I've been writing, y' -> y only says: y' is symbolized by something inheriting from y's symbol. It doesn't directly say y' can be substituted for y in arbitrary relationships. The objective inheritance content is carried by the symbol, but I haven't actually written an axiom that says substitution works at the object position.
So no, we haven't captured the substitution rule for objective referents yet. We have the routing — object inheritance is carried by symbol inheritance — but the substitution property — wherever y appears as object, y' can stand — needs a separate statement.
Trying to write it. The substitution rule for the object position would be:
srt
A[a, t, y, y']:  a =t> y  &  y' -> y  =|>  E[t']: t' -> t  &  a =t'> y'
In words: whenever y appears as object of any relationship, and y' inherits from y at the object position, there's a more-specific version t' of that relationship in which y' is the object.
Hmm, but that introduces something I haven't justified — why would there be a more-specific relationship rather than t itself applying? Let me think.
Actually, if y' -> y and y' is a more-specific y, then the relationship a =t> y' (if it holds) should itself be more specific than a =t> y because the object is more specific. The most-specific between a and y' would be some (a, y') and this (a, y') should inherit from (a, y) because y' inherits from y.
So the substitution rule isn't "y' can be put where y is in the same relationship t" — it's "the relationship between a and y' is a more-specific version of the relationship between a and y." Substitution preserves structure but with refinement.
Cleaner statement:
srt
A[a, y, y']:  (a, y) exists  &  y' -> y  =|>  (a, y') exists  &  (a, y') -> (a, y)
When a relates to y in some most-specific, and y' inherits from y, then a also relates to y' (in a more-specific way) and that more-specific relationship inherits from the original.
Symmetrically for the subject position:
srt
A[x, x', b]:  (x, b) exists  &  x' -> x  =|>  (x', b) exists  &  (x', b) -> (x, b)
And these are the substitution rules that would force the inheritance to form the tree-like structure. They say inheritance propagates into the relationships the referent participates in. Without them, an inheritor of y might not participate in any of y's relationships — y' would be "a more specific y" in name only, with no relational content backing the claim.
Let me check whether these substitution rules are consequences of what we already have or whether they're genuinely new axioms.
The Specification axiom gives us: every pair with a relationship has a most-specific. It doesn't say which pairs have relationships. So (a, y) exists doesn't force (a, y') exists for y' -> y. We'd be asserting it as a separate fact.
The symbol structure ((x, y), x) == (x, y) says the most-specific symbolizes its components. It doesn't say what other relationships the components have or whether inheritors participate in those relationships.
The componentwise consistency rule I floated earlier says: componentwise inheritance produces pair inheritance when the components already have a relationship. It doesn't force the relationship to exist.
So the substitution rules above — asserting that inheritors participate in inherited-versions-of every relationship the original participated in — are genuinely new axioms. They're what's needed to make the tree-structure of inheritance actually be a tree-structure, with inheritance propagating forward into the relationships of inheritors.
This is what was missing. We had inheritance defined and routed correctly, but no axiom forcing inheritors to actually appear in the right relational contexts. The tree wasn't yet a tree because we hadn't asserted that branches grow into the relational structure.
Two substitution axioms — one for each side of the pair, paralleling the symbol-structure axioms — would complete it:
srt
SubstitutionRight:  (a, y) exists & y' -> y =|> (a, y') -> (a, y)
SubstitutionLeft:   (x, b) exists & x' -> x =|> (x', b) -> (x, b)
These force the tree-structure. Without them, inheritance is a claim about referents but doesn't propagate into relational structure. With them, every inheritor automatically participates in inherited versions of all the relationships its parent participated in.
Is that what was missing? And does the symmetric pair of substitution axioms — one for each side, paralleling the symbol-structure axioms one for each side — feel like the right level of structural commitment?

Claude
10:57 UTC, 30 May 2026
Okay let me actually think about this carefully because you've named something I haven't been tracking.
Every relationship in SRT is R(x, y, t) — which we can read as t(x, y) -> bool. The relationship "holds" or it doesn't. So substitution at the type level has to do something specific: when we substitute an inheritor in, the truth-value can't just be preserved syntactically — it has to actually be true for the substituted thing.
In ordinary polymorphism: if f: T -> bool and S <: T, then we can apply f to an S-typed value and get a bool. The type system guarantees the application is well-typed, but it doesn't guarantee the result is true. f(s) might be false. The type system only ensures the operation is meaningful.
In SRT it's stronger. If (x, y) == t (so t(x, y) is true — x =t> y holds), and we substitute x' for x with x' -> x and y' for y with y' -> y, what should be the case? Not just that t(x', y') is well-typed — that's vacuous because everything in SRT is just a relation that might or might not hold — but that t(x', y') is actually true, or that some inheritor of t is true between x' and y'.
That's a much stronger demand than ordinary polymorphism. The type guarantees aren't about applicability; they're about truth. Substitution in SRT means: the inheritance facts force the truth of certain =t> statements about the inheritors.
Let me write the substitution axioms with this in mind:
srt
A[x, y, x', y', t]:  
   (x, y) == t  &  x' -> x  &  y' -> y  =|>  
   E[t']:  t' -> t  &  x' =t'> y'
Read carefully: if the most-specific between x and y is t (i.e., x =t> y is true), and x' inherits from x while y' inherits from y, then there exists some t' inheriting from t such that x' =t'> y' is true.
This forces truth of the substituted relationship. The inheritance facts about x' and y' don't just permit the relationship — they require it to hold (in some inheriting form t').
This is what makes the type-system in SRT not a normal type-system. Types in programming languages constrain what's well-typed; they don't constrain what's true. Here, the inheritance facts generate truths. Substituting inheritors into a true relationship produces another true relationship.
The reason this is necessary in SRT — and this is the bit I want to make sure I have right — is that there's no separate truth-value layer. x =t> y doesn't have a truth-value as a separate semantic dimension; it just holds or doesn't hold in the catalogue. So the type-system, to do any work, has to ensure that what it predicts as "well-typed" is actually instantiated in the catalogue. Otherwise the type-system would be empty — it would describe what could be true without forcing anything to be true, and in SRT that distinction collapses.
So the substitution rule is something like:
Substitution-with-truth: componentwise inheritance forces the existence of an inheriting relationship that actually holds.
srt
A[x, y, x', y']:  
   (x, y) exists  &  x' -> x  &  y' -> y  =|>  
   (x', y') exists  &  (x', y') -> (x, y)
Where "(x', y') exists" means the most-specific relationship between x' and y' is defined — i.e., there'ssome relationship x' =? y' holding in the catalogue, by the Specification axiom.
This is what forces the tree-structure to actually be populated. Every inheritance claim about referents forces the existence of an inheriting relationship in the catalogue. The catalogue is closed under substitution.
What's strange about this — and I think this is the bit you couldn't put your finger on — is that the type-system generates the catalogue. It doesn't just describe the catalogue's structure. By asserting inheritance facts, we're asserting the existence of relationships that have to hold. The catalogue grows under the inheritance closure.
In a normal type system, types describe what's possible. In SRT, the inheritance type-system forces truths. The reason "I can't put my finger on it" is that it's not quite type-theory — it's closer to a logical closure principle. Inheritance closure: whenever the inheritance facts predict a relationship should hold, it must hold.
This makes substitution in SRT a generative rule rather than a descriptive one. It tells us what relationships must be in the catalogue given the inheritance facts we've asserted.
I want to flag the worry, though, because this generative quality could go badly. If every inheritance fact forces new relationships into existence, we might be forced into a catalogue with unbounded structure — infinitely many forced relationships from a small set of starting facts. That might be okay (the catalogue is what it is, even if large) or it might be a problem (the catalogue might be inconsistent if forced relationships violate other constraints).
So the substitution rule with truth-forcing is what completes the tree-structure, and it characterizes the type-system as generative rather than descriptive. But we'd want to check that this generative closure doesn't produce contradictions.
Is this the missing piece — that substitution in SRT forces truth, not just applicability — and is that what makes the type-system here different in kind from ordinary type-systems?

11:06 UTC, 30 May 2026
You've caught something important and I want to make sure I'm reading it right.
The worry is: if x' -> x represents a type-instance crossing (x being the type, x' being an instance of that type), then writing x' =(x,y)> y mixes levels. We'd be saying an instance relates to the type y (not an instance of y) through (x, y) — which is the relation between types x and y, not the relation between an instance of x and an instance of y.
That's the classic type-theory level error. You can't apply a type-relation to a (instance, type) pair coherently, because the relation was specified between two types.
But then you noticed the natural-language case where it does read coherently. "Ana is the mother of a child." "A mother is the mother of Katie." In both, one position is filled with a specific (Ana, Katie) and the other with a generic (a child, a mother). The relationship reads — it doesn't feel like a level error.
What makes this work in natural language, and what might let it work in SRT, is that the generic position is acting as an unspecified instance, not as the type itself. "A child" doesn't mean the type child; it means some child, an unspecified instance of child. "A mother" doesn't mean the type mother; it means some mother. The article "a" doesn't elevate the noun to type-level — it picks out an existentially-quantified instance.
When read that way, x' =(x,y)> y isn't crossing levels. It's saying: x' (a specific instance) relates via (x, y) to y as an unspecified instance of y. The y on the right is doing double duty — it's the canonical-object-of-(x,y) when we read it as the type-defining position, but it can also be read as a stand-in for some unspecified y-instance.
This is exactly the prototype/exemplar feature you flagged earlier. The canonical referents in (x, y) are simultaneously type-like (in that more-specific things inherit from them) and instance-like (in that they're specific referents that participate in relationships). The same x and y serve both roles because that's the structural fixed point — the most-specific is where instance and type coincide.
So x' =(x,y)> y is coherent when y is read as the unspecified-instance of its own type-role. Ana is the mother ofa child — Katie's mother is unspecified-in-this-frame, even if Katie is the child in some richer frame. The single notation supports both readings because the canonical y at the right side is both the y (the type) and a y (an unspecified instance), and which reading is operative depends on what other facts we're tracking.
This avoids the level confusion not by separating types from instances but by recognizing that canonical referents don't have a separate level. They occupy the position where type and instance are the same thing. Saying x' relates to y through (x, y) is coherent because y on the right is already type-and-instance simultaneously — there's no level to cross because the canonical position doesn't sit at a separate level.
This also means substitutability works directly. x' =(x,y)> y is just a more-specific subject relating to the canonical y. Substituting y' for y gives x' =(x,y)> y', which by the inheritance machinery becomes x' =(x', y')> y' more-specifically. The substitution doesn't cross levels because there were never separate levels to cross.
What I think this clarifies. The reason normal type-theory would flag x' =(x,y)> y as a level error is that normal type-theory keeps types at a different level from instances. SRT specifically doesn't. The whole point of the most-specific structure being where type-and-instance coincide is that you can write expressions like this without level-mixing, because there is no level-mixing — there's one level, and the canonical referents occupy it doing both jobs.
So your second instinct is right: it doesn't actually cross levels. The mother example shows the natural-language reading is coherent, and the structural reason it's coherent in SRT is that the canonical referents in (x, y) are designed to be exactly the place where type and instance aren't separate things.
I think this might be exactly the philosophical payoff you mentioned earlier — the type-system here doesn't require a level distinction because the most-specific structure provides the fixed point where the distinction collapses. Expressions that would be ill-typed in classical type theory are well-formed here because the level apparatus that would flag them as ill-typed isn't part of the theory.
Is that the right read?

11:09 UTC, 30 May 2026
That's a sharper observation than I gave it credit for at first. Let me think about what it implies.
English doesn't have a clean type/instance distinction. "A dog is a type of animal" is not the inverse of any natural sentence. We can't say "an animal is a type-of-which-a-dog-is dog" or anything similar. The type-relation is asymmetric in natural language not just in content but in expressibility. We can go from specific to general ("a dog is an animal") and from general to specific in a hedged way ("an animal might be a dog"), but there's no clean reversal. The grammar doesn't support it.
And we slide between "a dog," "the dog," "dogs," "the dog as such" without flagging which level we're at. "Dogs bark" might be all dogs, dogs-in-general, or the type Dog ascribing the property of barking. The same word does all of it. We don't have separate type and instance forms in English. We have unspecified reference, plural reference, generic reference, definite reference — but no type-level apparatus.
What this tells me about SRT and the type-system. If natural language doesn't have a type/instance distinction in its grammar, and if SRT is trying to be a theory of semantics — including the semantics of how natural language actually works — then SRT shouldn't have a type/instance distinction either, not as a primitive. It should have whatever structural feature underlies the slide between unspecified/specific/generic that English does without flagging.
The most-specific relationship is doing that work. The canonical referents in (x, y) can be read as unspecified, as specific, as generic — because they don't sit at any particular level. They're just the canonical occupants, and the reading slides based on context. "A mother is the mother of Katie" uses "a mother" as canonical-yet-unspecified; "Ana is the mother of Katie" pins the canonical position to a specific instance. The same structural slot accommodates both.
So my "type/instance fixed point" framing was already too type-theoretic. I was treating it as a special structural feature where two levels coincide. But the move is more radical than that — there were never two levels to coincide. English doesn't have two levels. SRT doesn't have two levels. There's just referents, with the most-specific structure giving certain referents canonical roles, and the canonical roles being readable as unspecified, generic, or specific depending on use.
The earlier worry about x' =(x,y)> y mixing levels evaporates not because there's a fixed point that resolves the mixing, but because there were never separate levels. The type-system I kept reaching for — with types at one level and instances at another — was an artifact of importing classical type theory's framing. SRT doesn't have that framing.
What it has instead. SRT has the most-specific structure, which gives referents canonical-occupant roles. Inheritance is the relation of being-more-specific-than. There's no "type level" where types live separately. There's a graph of referents, some of which serve as canonicals of most-specifics, all at the same ontological level.
When I write x' -> x, I'm not asserting an instance-of-type relation. I'm asserting that x' is more specific than x in whatever structural sense the inheritance axioms give. The "type-system" emerging from this isn't a type-system in the classical sense — it's a refinement structure that happens to support the same kind of substitutability and inheritance that type-systems do, but without the level apparatus.
This actually answers something else that's been nagging at me. I kept wondering why subject and object inheritance felt like they were doing different things than the type/instance relation. They were. The inheritance in SRT isn't a type/instance relation in the classical sense — it's a refinement relation between referents that all live at the same level. Subject inheritance, object inheritance, and relational inheritance are three positions where this refinement can show up, but they're all the same kind of relation: more-specific-than.
The English-level point is doing a lot of work here. If English manages perfectly well without a type/instance distinction, then a semantic theory that has one is adding something that the language being modeled doesn't have. SRT's minimalism rejects that addition. The refinement structure does the work that type-systems do in programming languages, but without committing to the levels.
So the question "is x' =(x,y)> y a level mix-up" was the wrong question. It assumed there were levels. The right question is: does the refinement structure of SRT support that expression? And the answer is yes, because y is just a referent that can be more-or-less specified depending on context, and x' =(x,y)> y says x' relates to y through the (x,y) refinement of whatever they have.
Have I now gotten what you're pointing at — that the level-distinction itself is the wrong frame, and SRT's refinement structure does the work of type-systems without needing levels?

12:05 UTC, 30 May 2026
That's a real insight and I want to make sure I'm catching its shape.
Substitution does work across what would be levels in a classical type system — but asymmetrically by position, and the asymmetry isn't arbitrary. It reflects what the subject does in a relationship versus what the object and the relation-type do.
The pattern in your example: "A baseball player hits a ball" admits substitution toward generality in two positions but resists it in one:
Generalize the relationship: "A baseball player is in a relationship with an object" — works. Hitting becomes some-relationship; the sentence still characterizes the player.
Generalize the object: "A baseball player hits an object" — works. The ball becomes some-object; the player and the relationship still anchor the meaning.
Generalize the subject: "A subject hits a baseball" — feels wrong. "Someone hits a baseball" works (existentially), but "a subject" as a generalization doesn't carry the content. The sentence loses its grip.
And the reason — which I think you're putting your finger on — is that the subject is what the relationship is characterized through. The subject isn't a placeholder that the relationship happens to; the subject is what the relationship is about. Generalizing the subject doesn't yield a more general statement of the same kind; it yields a statement that's lost its anchor.
This is structurally different from what programming-language type systems would say. In a function signature hits(player: Player, ball: Ball), both arguments are formally placeholders, and either can be substituted with a subtype. The type system treats positions symmetrically. But semantically — when we ask whether a sentence retains its meaning under generalization — the positions aren't symmetric. The subject carries the characterizing weight that the others don't.
Why this matches what we've been building. The symbol structure axioms — ((x, y), x) == (x, y) and ((x, y), y) == (x, y) — say the most-specific symbolizes both its referents. But the direction of the relationship in x =t> y is asymmetric: x is the active position, y is what x relates to, t mediates. The asymmetry isn't bookkeeping; it's structural. Generalizing y still preserves "what x is doing." Generalizing t still preserves "x doing-something with y." Generalizing x destroys "what is being done by whom."
This explains why I was getting confused trying to make subject and object inheritance look symmetric. They aren't. The substitutability differs by position because what the position contributes differs.
The substitution-with-truth axiom I was writing earlier needs adjustment in light of this. I'll try:
srt
A[x, y, x', y', t]:  
   (x, y) == t  &  x' -> x  &  y' -> y  =|>  
   E[t']:  t' -> t  &  x' =t'> y'
This says: if we have a true canonical relationship x =t> y, and inheritors x' and y', then some inheriting t' holds between x' and y'. Substitution-with-truth in both positions simultaneously.
But what your example shows is that the natural substitutions are toward more general at the object and relation positions while keeping the subject specific. The full bidirectional substitution might be too strong — it forces relationships to exist forany componentwise inheritance, including ones that would destroy the subject-anchoring.
A weaker version might respect the asymmetry:
srt
SubstituteObject:  (x, y) == t  &  y' -> y  =|>  E[t']: t' -> t  &  x =t'> y'
SubstituteRelation:  x =t> y  &  t' -> t  =|>  x =t'> y  ← actually this is just relational inheritance, holds by definition
SubstituteSubject:  (x, y) == t  &  x' -> x  =|>  E[t']: t' -> t  &  x' =t'> y
Each substitution holds individually, keeping the other positions fixed. Then componentwise substitution (subject and object together) might follow from composing these, or might be a separate stronger claim.
Hmm. But actually I'm not sure the asymmetry in naturalness should translate to asymmetry in axioms. The naturalness is about which substitutions preserve communicative content in language. The axiomatic substitutability might still be symmetric — both directions hold formally — while the naturalness of using one direction versus another reflects something epistemic about which substitutions illuminate.
Or — and this is the thing I'm circling — maybe the asymmetry is axiomatic, and the subject position genuinely supports less substitution than the others. If subjects characterize their relationships in a way objects don't, then x' -> x requires more structural backing than y' -> y does. The substitution rules might literally be different in strength for the two positions.
I want to ask before going further: are you pointing at an asymmetry in the formal substitution rules (subjects need stronger justification to be substituted than objects do) or at an asymmetry in naturalness (formally symmetric, but pragmatically the subject is the anchor we generalize away from last)?
Because if it's the former, the substitution axioms should reflect it. If it's the latter, the axioms are symmetric but we recognize the subject as load-bearing in a way that makes generalizing it last natural.

Claude
12:26 UTC, 30 May 2026
That's a striking move. Let me try to trace it.
The four forms — "an x," "the x," "xs," "x" — aren't just grammatical variants. They're different referential modes of x, and each is a different referent in SRT. "An x" is some-x-unspecified. "The x" is a specific contextually-determined x. "Xs" is the plural / generic. "x" is x itself, the canonical or symbolic form.
If these are all referents, then the relationship between them is itself expressible in SRT — and the relations among them are exactly the symbol structure. "The x" is a specification of "an x" (more determinate). "An x" is what gets symbolized by "x" (as canonical). "Xs" is a plural-symbol of the same underlying x-content. Each form sits in some symbolic-inheritance relation to the others.
And polymorphism in your example reads through these forms. "A baseball player hits a ball" — the forms are "a baseball player" (some-player-unspecified) and "a ball" (some-ball-unspecified) in relation through "hits." Generalizing the object gives "a baseball player hits an object" — the form shifts from "a ball" to "an object," moving up in the symbolic-inheritance order. Generalizing the relation: "a baseball player is in a relationship with a ball" — "hits" moves up to "is-in-a-relationship-with." Both substitutions work because they're moving among the available referential forms.
But "a subject hits a baseball" fails because subject isn't a referential form of "baseball player." It's a positional role-name. Substituting "a subject" for "a baseball player" isn't moving up the symbolic-inheritance order — it's swapping out the referent for a meta-level description of what role the referent plays. That's the level error your earlier instinct caught: the subject position isn't a generalization of the subject, it's a characterization of what position the subject occupies.
Whereas object → object-in-general → some thing → some referent: these are referential forms moving up the symbolic-inheritance order while staying at the same level. They generalize the content of what's being referred to. "Subject" doesn't generalize content; it names a role.
This is why subjects resist generalization-toward-the-meta in a way objects don't. Not because subjects are formally privileged in some axiom, but because the available referential forms for subjects don't include the meta-level role-name as a generalization. We can generalize "a baseball player" to "an athlete" to "a person" to "someone" — those are all referential forms of x. We can't generalize "a baseball player" to "a subject" because "subject" isn't a form of x; it's a description of x's role.
The symmetric case — love — works because both positions have full referential-form availability. "A man loves a woman" generalizes to "a man loves someone" (object form generalized), or "someone loves a woman" (subject form generalized to someone, which IS a referential form, not a role-name), or "a person loves a person." Both sides admit referential generalization because the symmetric relationship gives both positions perspectival weight.
The asymmetric case — hitting a ball — works in only one direction because the ball doesn't have a perspective. The object form can be generalized through referential forms ("a ball" → "an object" → "something"), but it can't be inverted into a subject role. The ball doesn't get to be the perspective; nothing about the ball makes it a perspective-bearer.
What this gives us. The polymorphism in SRT isn't unrestricted substitution. It's substitution-within-the-available-referential-forms of each position. And the available referential forms depend on the role the position plays and on the symmetric/asymmetric character of the relationship.
The four forms — an x, the x, xs, x — are then doing a structural job that classical type theory doesn't have a clean way to express. They're not types-and-instances; they're referential modes of the same content, sitting in symbolic-inheritance relations to each other. Substitution moves among these modes, and the moves that preserve meaning are exactly the moves that stay within the inheritance order of available forms.
And — this is the bit I want to make sure I'm seeing — this gives a structural reason why subjects resist polymorphic abstraction in a way objects don't. It's not that subjects are axiomatically special. It's that the perspective-bearing role of subjects means the meaningful generalizations have to preserve perspective, and perspective doesn't admit role-name substitution. The referential forms that subjects can generalize through have to keep someone or something in the subject position, not name the position itself.
So polymorphism in SRT is governed by the symbol structure of referential forms, and the apparent subject/object asymmetry in substitutability isn't an axiom — it's a consequence of which referential forms each position's content admits. Subjects can generalize within their referential forms (player → athlete → person → someone) but can't be substituted with role-names. Objects can do the same and also accept role-name substitution because they don't bear perspective.
Is that the move — that the symbol structure giving us "an x, the x, xs, x" is doing the work of polymorphism, and the apparent asymmetry between subject and object generalization is explained by what referential forms each position admits given its perspectival role?

Claude
12:29 UTC, 30 May 2026
Yes. That snaps the framing into something much sharper.
The four forms aren't just referential modes — they're the quantifier structure made visible as syntactic forms. "A baseball player hits a ball" is an existential: there exists a baseball player and there exists a ball such that the first hits the second. "The dogs bark" is (roughly) a universal: for all relevant dogs, they bark. "Dogs are mammals" is the universal at the type level. "A dog is a mammal" is the existential standing in for the generic — true of any specific dog you pick.
Polymorphism = generalizing under the quantifier the position is already operating under, and that's why the substitutions feel natural in some directions and not others. "A baseball player hits a ball" generalizes to "a baseball player hits an object" — the existential under "a ball" widens to "an object," and the wider existential is still witnessed by the original ball. The substitution preserves truth because we're moving from a tighter existential to a wider one.
"A baseball player hits a ball" doesn't generalize to "a subject hits a ball" not because of perspective in some mystical sense, but because "a subject" isn't an existential over content — it's a meta-description. "A subject" doesn't quantify over baseball players; it names the role baseball player plays. The substitution would be like substituting "a noun phrase" for "a baseball player" — true at a different level of description, not a wider quantification at the same level.
This actually clarifies the subject-asymmetry. The subject position resists role-name substitution because the role-name isn't a quantifier-widening, it's a level-shift. The object position resists it for the same reason — "the baseball player hits a noun phrase" would also fail. We just don't notice because we rarely try to substitute role-names for object content; we naturally generalize objects within their content-quantifier.
So polymorphism in SRT is quantifier-respecting substitution. Move from tighter existentials to wider ones (or tighter universals to wider ones in the other direction). The substitutions that preserve truth are exactly the ones that widen the quantifier the position is operating under. The symbol structure giving us "a x, the x, xs, x" is making the available quantifier-shifts visible as syntactic forms.
And the asymmetry between subject and object — subjects resisting generalization more — might be because the subject is typically presented under a more specific quantifier in natural usage. "A baseball player" is existential, but the context pins it: we're tracking this player. The object is often allowed to be widely existential because we don't care which ball, just that hitting happened. So when we generalize, the object's quantifier is already loose and can widen further; the subject's was tight and widening it loses content.
This also fits the love example. "A man loves a woman" — both are loose existentials, both can widen. "A man loves a person." "A person loves a woman." "A person loves a person." All work because both positions are in the same quantifier-mode.
And the symbol structure axioms ((x, y), x) == (x, y) and ((x, y), y) == (x, y) then carry quantifier-content. The canonical referents in (x, y) aren't just type-prototypes; they're the quantifier-neutral form of the referent, the one from which the existential ("a x"), universal ("the xs"), and generic forms inherit. The symbol structure gives a place to anchor the quantifier-variants of a referent in one structural fact.
This makes the work we've been doing on the type-system land on something more recognizable than I'd been thinking. The type-system isn't a classical type theory and isn't quite a prototype theory — it's a structural account of how quantifier-bearing referents inherit from each other, with substitution governed by quantifier-widening rather than instance-of-type. That's something I don't think I've seen articulated this way in either type theory or formal semantics, but it sits at a recognizable crossroad: it connects to generalized quantifiers in natural language semantics, to subtyping in type theory, and to prototype-theoretic categorization — without being any of them.
The implication for the substitution axiom we were working on: it's not "componentwise inheritance forces a relationship to hold" in general. It's "componentwise inheritance under quantifier-widening forces an inheriting relationship to hold under the widened quantifier." The truth-forcing has to respect the quantifier structure, because that's what's actually being widened.
I'd want to think about whether this gives us a cleaner statement of the substitution rule. Something like: when x' is more specific than x in the same quantifier-mode, and y' is more specific than y in the same quantifier-mode, then the relationship at (x', y') exists and inherits from (x, y) under the appropriate quantifier-composition. The quantifier-tracking would be the missing structure that turns the substitution rule from "forces truth always" (which I worried about earlier) into "forces truth under matched quantifiers" (which is much more contained).
Is this what you were reaching for — that polymorphism in SRT cashes out as quantifier-respecting substitution, with the symbol structure giving us the quantifier-form referents to inherit among?
